Sections \ref{dplyrworkflow} served as a capstone---bringing together the preceding concepts and functions into workflows to explore the Minnesota tree growth datasets introduced in Sections \ref{minnesota}.

Sections \ref{twoTables} turns to functions for combining two tibbles. Mutating joins, introduced in Section \ref{mutatingJoins}, use matching rows to add columns from the second tibble to the first tibble. Filtering joins, introduced in Section \ref{filteringJoins}, subset rows in the first tibble given matching rows in the second tibble. Filtering joins are typically used to diagnose row mismatch issues prior to performing mutating joins. In Section \ref{allometricJoins}, we stepped through a process to estimate tree height and volume using species-specific allometric equations. The process involved a series of filtering and mutating joins that created a single tibble that included all the columns needed to create the desired height and volume columns via calls to \texttt{mutate()}.

A suite of set operations were reviewed in Section \ref{setOperations}. These functions operate on tibbles with the same columns, and are used most often to identify unique or common rows between tibbles, or to combine tibbles while removing duplicate rows. Finally, Section \ref{binds} introduced the \texttt{bind\_rows()} and \texttt{bind\_cols()} functions used to augment tibble rows and columns, respectively.

\hypertarget{exercisesDplyr}{%
\section{Exercises}\label{exercisesDplyr}}

Figure \ref{fig:dplyrExerciseData} provides timber cruise data collected over three inventory plots and recorded in a field notebook. Rows in the notebook correspond to measurement trees and columns are defined as follows.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{Plot} plot number on which the tree was measured.
\item
  \texttt{Spp} species code.
\item
  \texttt{DBH} DBH in centimeters.
\item
  \texttt{Ht} total tree height in meters.
\item
  \texttt{Status} live (L) or dead (D).
\end{enumerate}



\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{figures/dplyr_tally_data_example} 

}

\caption{Example timber cruise data collected over three inventory plots and used for \texttt{dplyr} exercises.}\label{fig:dplyrExerciseData}
\end{figure}

Data provided in Figure \ref{fig:dplyrExerciseData} are used for Exercises \ref{exr:dplyr1} through \ref{exr:dplyrLast}. For most exercises, we provide the desired output and it's your job to write the \texttt{dplyr} code to reproduce the output. Use the pipe operator when possible.

\begin{exercise}
\protect\hypertarget{exr:dplyr21}{}\label{exr:dplyr21}Next, let's add stem volume estimates using the tree measurements. These data were collected in costal British Columbia, Canada. Given we have species, DBH (cm), and total height (m), we can look for models that take these variables as input and return volume (ft\(^3\)). A suitable model for this setting is defined as
\begin{equation}
 \text{volume} = \exp(\beta_0) + \text{DBH}^{\beta_1} + \text{Ht}^{b_2},
 \label{eq:nigh}
\end{equation}
where \(\beta_0\), \(\beta_1\), and \(\beta_2\) regression coefficient estimates for merchantable volume are provided in Table 6 of \citet{Nigh2016}. For convenience, we copied these coefficients from \citet{Nigh2016} to ``datasets/Nigh\_coefficients.csv''.

Add a new column to \texttt{plots} called \texttt{vol\_ft3} that holds merchantable volume for each tree and do some clean up when you're done. Write the code to replicate the following steps.
\end{exercise}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi})}
\tightlist
\item
  Make a coefficients tibble by reading in the species specific regression coefficients held in ``Nigh\_coefficients.csv''. Hint, open ``Nigh\_coefficients.csv'' in a text editor to see what it contains and how the columns are delimited.
\item
  Add these regression coefficients as new columns to \texttt{plots} via a join by \texttt{plots} column \texttt{Spp} and coefficients tibble \texttt{species\_code}.
\item
  Specify \eqref{eq:nigh} within \texttt{mutate()} to add the desired \texttt{vol\_ft3} column. Place \texttt{vol\_ft3} to the right of \texttt{Ht\_m}.
\item
  Remove extraneous columns from \texttt{plots} (i.e., remove \texttt{common\_name}, \texttt{scientific\_name}, \texttt{species\_code}, \texttt{beta\_0}, \texttt{beta\_1}, \texttt{beta\_2}).
\end{enumerate}

Your resulting \texttt{plots} should look like the following.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{plots}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 7 x 7
#>    Plot Spp   DBH_cm BA_m2  Ht_m vol_ft3 Live 
#>   <int> <chr>  <dbl> <dbl> <dbl>   <dbl> <lgl>
#> 1     1 WH      95   49.2   47.5   4092. TRUE 
#> 2     1 FD     106.  61.9   49     2959. TRUE 
#> 3     2 SS      68   25.2   38     1996. TRUE 
#> 4     2 SS      35    6.68  20      617. TRUE 
#> 5     2 PL      NA   NA     NA       NA  FALSE
#> 6     3 PW      52.5 15.0   33     1718. TRUE 
#> 7     3 PL      34    6.31  26.5    779. TRUE
\end{verbatim}

\hypertarget{tidyr}{%
\chapter{\texorpdfstring{Tidying data with \texttt{tidyr}}{Tidying data with tidyr}}\label{tidyr}}

Formats used to organize data are typically chosen to simplify recording, storage, analysis, or presentation. As we'll see, a format ideal for one purpose might not be ideal for other purposes. For example, a format useful for recording values in the field might not lend itself well to subsequent analysis or succinct data summaries.

The package \texttt{tidyr} provides functions to simplify moving between dataset formats and other common data wrangling and analysis tasks. Let's align our definitions for dataset components with those used in \texttt{tidyr}. In \texttt{tidyr}, a \emph{dataset} is a collection of values, i.e., numbers, character strings and other data types we covered. Every value is indexed by a variable and an observation. A variable contains all values that measure the same underlying characteristic (e.g., DBH, height, volume, or species) across observations. An observation (e.g., tree or plot) contains all values measured across variables.

Following \citet{wickham2023}, \emph{tidy} data is a way to map a dataset's meaning to its structure. A dataset is messy or tidy depending on how rows and columns are matched with observations, variables, and values. Tidy data meet the following conditions.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Every column is a variable.
\item
  Every row is an observation.
\item
  Every row/column element is a single value.
\end{enumerate}

Data are \emph{messy} if they do not meet one or more of the tidy data conditions. Tidy data simplify many data summary and analysis tasks because they provide a standard way for structuring a dataset.

Consider the left dataset in Figure \ref{fig:pivotFig} called \texttt{dbh\_wide}. Rows of \texttt{dbh\_wide} correspond to three trees, columns are measurement years, and values are DBH measurements. As the dataset name suggests, these data are in \emph{wide format}\index{Data format!wide}. This dataset is also messy because more than one column holds DBH measurements and column names themselves are values for a year variable. As we'll explore further in Section \ref{tidyrReshape}, \texttt{tidyr\textquotesingle{}}s \texttt{pivot\_longer()} function changes wide format to \emph{long format}\index{Data format!long}---a process illustrated by moving from \texttt{trees\_wide} to \texttt{trees\_long} in Figure \ref{fig:pivotFig}. The \texttt{trees\_long} dataset is tidy, as each column holds only one variable (i.e., \texttt{id}, \texttt{year}, and \texttt{dbh}) and each row corresponds to a single \texttt{id}, \texttt{year}, and \texttt{dbh} observation.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{figures/tidyr_pivot} 

}

\caption{Changing longitudinal DBH measurements on three trees from wide to long format.}\label{fig:pivotFig}
\end{figure}

Most datasets we encounter are messy. Importantly, messy data are not bad or wrong, in fact a messy table is often a very effective way to summarize data as we'll see in Section \ref{standStockTabs}. However, from a data analysis standpoint, tidy data often simplify analysis and conversion to other formats. Receiving data in a messy format typically means you'll spend some time wrangling it into either a tidy format or another messy format to meet your needs. Subsequent sections cover some \texttt{tidyr} functions used to clean up messy data. Specifically, we'll cover how to remedy the following messy data features.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Column headers are values, not variable names.
\item
  Multiple variables are stored in one column.
\item
  Variables are stored in both rows and columns.
\end{itemize}

The subsequent sections illustrate several \texttt{tidyr} functions using the messy FACE dataset introduced in Section \ref{face}. As a refresher, the Aspen FACE Experiment was designed to assess the effects of increasing tropospheric ozone and carbon dioxide concentrations on the structure and functioning of northern forest ecosystems. The code below loads packages we'll use in our tidying efforts and reads the FACE data into the \texttt{face} tibble.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\KeywordTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\KeywordTok{library}\NormalTok{(tidyr)}

\NormalTok{face \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"datasets/FACE/FACE\_aspen\_core\_growth.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Run \texttt{face\ \%\textgreater{}\%\ glimpse()} to see column names and types. Also notice non-syntactic column names in the ``FACE\_aspen\_core\_growth.csv'' file are placed within backticks in the \texttt{face} tibble.\footnote{See Section \ref{syntacticNames} for syntactic name rules.}

In the next few sections, the messy FACE dataset is used to introduce some \texttt{tidyr} functions. We focus on a subset of the FACE dataset that includes each tree's identifiers and height measurements for 2001 through 2005. The code below uses \texttt{dplyr} functions to select the desired columns, rename the \texttt{\textasciigrave{}ID\ \#\textasciigrave{}} column name to \texttt{ID}\footnote{As noted briefly in Section \ref{readr}, \texttt{readr} read functions make non-syntactic column names syntactic by surrounding them by backticks. While this nice feature preserves the external file's column names, it can be cumbersome to include backticks when referencing columns in subsequent workflows.}. In addition to unique tree \texttt{ID} and height measurements, we retain the experiment design variables replication (\texttt{Rep}), treatment (\texttt{Treat}), and tree clone identifier (\texttt{Clone}). Notice the use of the \texttt{contains()} helper function within \texttt{select()}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{face \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{face }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{select}\NormalTok{(Rep, Treat, Clone, }\DataTypeTok{ID =} \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\DataTypeTok{ID \#}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\NormalTok{,}
        \KeywordTok{contains}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{as.character}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2001}\OperatorTok{:}\DecValTok{2005}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{\&}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{contains}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Height"}\NormalTok{))}
         
\NormalTok{face}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 1,991 x 9
#>      Rep Treat Clone    ID `2001_Height` `2002_Height`
#>    <dbl> <dbl> <dbl> <dbl>         <dbl>         <dbl>
#>  1     1     1     8    45            NA            NA
#>  2     1     1   216    44           547           622
#>  3     1     1     8    43           273           275
#>  4     1     1   216    42           526           619
#>  5     1     1   216    54           328           341
#>  6     1     1   271    55           543           590
#>  7     1     1   271    56           450           502
#>  8     1     1     8    57           217           227
#>  9     1     1   259    58           158           155
#> 10     1     1   271    59           230           241
#> # i 1,981 more rows
#> # i 3 more variables: `2003_Height` <dbl>,
#> #   `2004_Height` <dbl>, `2005_Height` <dbl>
\end{verbatim}

\hypertarget{tidyrReshape}{%
\section{Wide and long formats}\label{tidyrReshape}}

Recall the FACE dataset is longitudinal, meaning measurements were taken repeatedly across time (in this case years). Notice in the \texttt{face} tibble, height measurements are stored in successive columns, i.e., \texttt{\textasciigrave{}2001\_Height\textasciigrave{}}\(\ldots\)\texttt{\textasciigrave{}2005\_Height\textasciigrave{}}. Like \texttt{trees\_wide} in Figure \ref{fig:pivotFig}, \texttt{face} is stored in wide format.

To tidy these messy data, our first task is to change \texttt{face} to a long format. We could imagine doing this in a spreadsheet software by copying and pasting, but this would be extremely tedious and would almost surely lead to errors. The \texttt{tidyr} package has two functions \texttt{pivot\_longer()} and \texttt{pivot\_wider()} to move between wide and long formats.

The call to \texttt{pivot\_longer()} below moves \texttt{face} from wide to long format. In the \texttt{pivot\_wider()} function, we specify the implicit \texttt{data\ =\ face}\footnote{To facilitate piping, \texttt{data} is the first argument in \texttt{tidyr} functions.}, columns to pivot into longer format (\texttt{cols\ =\ contains("Height")}), name to give the column created from the wide column names (\texttt{names\_to\ =\ "Year\_Type"}), and name we want to give to the column containing the measurements \texttt{values\_to\ =\ \textquotesingle{}Height\_cm\textquotesingle{}}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{face\_long \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{face }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{pivot\_longer}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{cols =} \KeywordTok{contains}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Height"}\NormalTok{),}
                                   \DataTypeTok{names\_to =} \StringTok{"Year\_Type"}\NormalTok{,}
                                   \DataTypeTok{values\_to =} \StringTok{"Height\_cm"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{face\_long}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 9,955 x 6
#>      Rep Treat Clone    ID Year_Type   Height_cm
#>    <dbl> <dbl> <dbl> <dbl> <chr>           <dbl>
#>  1     1     1     8    45 2001_Height        NA
#>  2     1     1     8    45 2002_Height        NA
#>  3     1     1     8    45 2003_Height        NA
#>  4     1     1     8    45 2004_Height        NA
#>  5     1     1     8    45 2005_Height        NA
#>  6     1     1   216    44 2001_Height       547
#>  7     1     1   216    44 2002_Height       622
#>  8     1     1   216    44 2003_Height       715
#>  9     1     1   216    44 2004_Height       716
#> 10     1     1   216    44 2005_Height       817
#> # i 9,945 more rows
\end{verbatim}

Let's compare the dimensions of the wide format \texttt{face} with \texttt{face\_long}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{dim}\NormalTok{(face)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> [1] 1991    9
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{dim}\NormalTok{(face\_long)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> [1] 9955    6
\end{verbatim}

There are more columns in the wide format \texttt{face} and more rows in the long format \texttt{face\_long}, hence the reason these formats are referred to as wide and long.

\texttt{pivot\_wider()} is used to widen a dataset in long format. For example, the code below moves \texttt{face\_long} back to its original wide format.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{face\_wide \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{face\_long }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{pivot\_wider}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{names\_from =} \StringTok{"Year\_Type"}\NormalTok{,}
          \DataTypeTok{values\_from =} \StringTok{"Height\_cm"}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Check if face\_wide and face are the same.}
\KeywordTok{all.equal}\NormalTok{(face\_wide, face)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> [1] TRUE
\end{verbatim}

In \texttt{pivot\_wider()} above, we specify the implicit \texttt{data\ =\ face\_long}, column from which to get wide column names (\texttt{names\_from\ =\ "Year\_Type"}), and column from which to get values (\texttt{values\_from\ =\ "Height\_cm"}). As expected, and shown using \texttt{all.equal(face\_wide,\ face)}, the call to \texttt{pivot\_wider()} returns a tibble that's the same as \texttt{face}.

\hypertarget{tidyrSeparate}{%
\section{Separating and uniting columns}\label{tidyrSeparate}}

Let's take another look at the \texttt{Year\_Type} column in the \texttt{face\_long} dataset.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{face\_long }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{select}\NormalTok{(Year\_Type)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 9,955 x 1
#>    Year_Type  
#>    <chr>      
#>  1 2001_Height
#>  2 2002_Height
#>  3 2003_Height
#>  4 2004_Height
#>  5 2005_Height
#>  6 2001_Height
#>  7 2002_Height
#>  8 2003_Height
#>  9 2004_Height
#> 10 2005_Height
#> # i 9,945 more rows
\end{verbatim}

The \texttt{Year\_Type} column contains two variables separated by an underscore---the measurement year and measurement type. To set \texttt{face} on the tidy data path, we need to separate \texttt{Year\_Type} into its components. Below, we use the \texttt{tidyr} \texttt{separate\_wider\_delim()} function to separate the \texttt{Year\_Type} column into \texttt{Year} and \texttt{Type}. We want to retain the \texttt{Year} component, whereas the \texttt{Type} component is simply the value \texttt{\textquotesingle{}Height"} replicated for each row and can be discarded.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{face\_long \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{face\_long }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{separate\_wider\_delim}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{cols =} \StringTok{"Year\_Type"}\NormalTok{,}
                       \DataTypeTok{delim =} \StringTok{"\_"}\NormalTok{,}
                       \DataTypeTok{names =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Year"}\NormalTok{, }\OtherTok{NA}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{face\_long}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 9,955 x 6
#>      Rep Treat Clone    ID Year  Height_cm
#>    <dbl> <dbl> <dbl> <dbl> <chr>     <dbl>
#>  1     1     1     8    45 2001         NA
#>  2     1     1     8    45 2002         NA
#>  3     1     1     8    45 2003         NA
#>  4     1     1     8    45 2004         NA
#>  5     1     1     8    45 2005         NA
#>  6     1     1   216    44 2001        547
#>  7     1     1   216    44 2002        622
#>  8     1     1   216    44 2003        715
#>  9     1     1   216    44 2004        716
#> 10     1     1   216    44 2005        817
#> # i 9,945 more rows
\end{verbatim}

The call to \texttt{separate\_wider\_delim()} above specifies the implicit \texttt{data\ =\ face\_long}, column to separate (\texttt{cols\ =\ "Year\_Type"}), delimiter that separates the columns (\texttt{delim\ =\ "\_"}), and names for the resulting columns (\texttt{names\ =\ c("Year",\ NA)}). The \texttt{NA} in the vector passed to the \texttt{names} argument means remove the column that corresponds to the \texttt{Type} component of the \texttt{Year\_Type} column. At this point \texttt{face\_long} is now tidy data!

Consult the manual page via \texttt{?tidyr::separate\_wider\_delim} to learn more about this and related functions for separating delimited columns.

There are occasions when you might want to combine multiple columns into one. We can do this using \texttt{unite()}. For example, the code below unites the \texttt{Rep}, \texttt{Treat}, and \texttt{Clone} columns into a single column called \texttt{Design}, with each of the values separated by an underscore. See \texttt{?tidyr::unite} for more details.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{face\_long }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{unite}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{col =} \StringTok{"Design"}\NormalTok{, Rep, Treat, Clone, }\DataTypeTok{sep =} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}\_\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 9,955 x 4
#>    Design     ID Year  Height_cm
#>    <chr>   <dbl> <chr>     <dbl>
#>  1 1_1_8      45 2001         NA
#>  2 1_1_8      45 2002         NA
#>  3 1_1_8      45 2003         NA
#>  4 1_1_8      45 2004         NA
#>  5 1_1_8      45 2005         NA
#>  6 1_1_216    44 2001        547
#>  7 1_1_216    44 2002        622
#>  8 1_1_216    44 2003        715
#>  9 1_1_216    44 2004        716
#> 10 1_1_216    44 2005        817
#> # i 9,945 more rows
\end{verbatim}

\hypertarget{standStockTabs}{%
\section{Stand and stock tables}\label{standStockTabs}}

Data collected for forest research, inventory, and monitoring efforts are typically summarized using a variety of tables. For example, \emph{stand} and \emph{stock} tables are key data summary products that support a variety of forest management decisions, e.g., silvicultural prescription, economic valuation, and structural diversity assessment. Recalling terminology introduced in Section \ref{typesOfVariables}, the stock table summarizes a \emph{quantitative discrete} variable (e.g., stem count) grouped by one or more \emph{categorical} (i.e., qualitative) variable (e.g., size class or species). Similarly, the stand table summarizes a \emph{quantitative continuous} variable (e.g., volume, weight, or basal area) grouped by one or more \emph{categorical} variable.

In forestry applications, stand tables often summarize the average number of trees per unit area (e.g., acre or hectare) within an area of interest (e.g., stand, management unit, or forest) by DBH class and species. Similarly, stock tables often summarize the average wood volume or weight per unit area within an area of interest by DBH class and species. DBH classes are DBH intervals with widths chosen to communicate situation specific information.

\begin{table}[!h]

\caption{\label{tab:stand1Ests}Estimates for number of trees and wood volume per acre by species and DBH used to illustrate building stand and stock tables.}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{cccc}
\toprule
Species & DBH (in) & Trees/ac & Volume (ft$^3$/ac)\\
\midrule
Abies balsamea & 11.3 & 8 & 142.8\\
Betula papyrifera & 14.8 & 8 & 269.6\\
Betula papyrifera & 15.4 & 8 & 293.7\\
Pinus strobus & 9.8 & 8 & 116.3\\
Pinus strobus & 10.7 & 8 & 143.6\\
Pinus strobus & 13.1 & 8 & 231.9\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

We motivate building stand and stock tables using forest stand estimates provided in Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests}. In this table, each row provides an estimated number of trees and volume on a per acre basis for a given species and DBH. For example, the first row in Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests} says a typical (or average) acre has 8 11.3 inch DBH \emph{Abies balsamea} stems with a combined volume of 142.8 ft\(^3\). We'll introduce the inventory data from which these estimates were derived later in Chapter \ref{tab:forestStat}. For now, however, these estimates are used to build the stand and stock tables given in Tables \ref{tab:stand1Stand} and \ref{tab:stand1Stock}, respectively.

Before getting started, notice stand and stock tables are not \emph{tidy} according to the definition given at the opening of this chapter---they are wide and messy, and that's okay. As we'll see below, it's best to start with long and tidy data, then change its format to the desired wide and messy stand or stock table.

Let's begin with the stand table shown in Table \ref{tab:stand1Stand}. Stand table rows correspond to the distinct species in Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests}, columns are four inch DBH classes, and values are the number of trees for the given row and column.

In Table \ref{tab:stand1Stand}, notice the parentheses and square bracket notation used to define DBH class intervals (i.e., column names). A \emph{closed interval}\index{Interval notation}, denoted by square brackets, means the endpoints are included in the interval. For example, \([6, 10]\) is a closed interval with 6 and 10 inch endpoints. Referencing Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests}, there is one \emph{Pinus strobus} with a DBH that falls within the \([6, 10]\) inch DBH class interval, hence the corresponding 8 trees/ac value in Table \ref{tab:stand1Stand}. A closed interval can also be written using \(\le\) notation, e.g., \([6, 10]=\{6 \le x \le 10\}\) where \(x\) is DBH. An \emph{open interval}, denoted by parentheses, means the endpoints are not included in the interval. Following from open and closed notation, a \emph{half-open} interval includes only one of its endpoints, and is denoted by one parentheses and one square bracket. The stand table has two half-open intervals \((10, 14]\) and \((14, 18]\) (these can be written as \(\{10 < x \le 14\}\) and \(\{14 < x \le 18\}\)). Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests} shows one \emph{Abies balsamea} and two \emph{Pinus strobus} in the \((10, 14]\) DBH class, and two \emph{Betula papyrifera} in the \((14, 18]\) DBH class. Stand table values are the sum of trees for the given species and DBH class, hence there are 16 \emph{Pinus strobus} in the \((10, 14]\) DBH class and 16 \emph{Betula papyrifera} in the \((14, 18]\) DBH class.

\begin{table}[!h]

\caption{\label{tab:stand1Stand}Stand table for estimates given in Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests}. Values are number of trees per acre.}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{ccccc}
\toprule
\multicolumn{1}{c}{ } & \multicolumn{3}{c}{DBH classes (in)} & \multicolumn{1}{c}{ } \\
\cmidrule(l{3pt}r{3pt}){2-4}
Species & {}[6,10] & (10,14] & (14,18] & Totals\\
\midrule
Abies balsamea & 0 & 8 & 0 & 8\\
Betula papyrifera & 0 & 0 & 16 & 16\\
Pinus strobus & 8 & 16 & 0 & 24\\
\cline{2-5}
Totals & 8 & 24 & 16 & 48\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The stock table is given in Table \ref{tab:stand1Stock}. This stock table is built the same way as the stand table, with the exception that its values are the sum of volume for each given species and DBH class. For example, the stand table's 8 \emph{Pinus strobus} trees per acre in the \([6, 8]\) inch DBH class, correspond to 116.3 ft\(^3\) of volume per acre, and the 16 \emph{Betula papyrifera} trees per acre in the \((14, 18]\) inch DBH class, correspond to 563.3 ft\(^3\) of volume per acre (i.e., \(269.6+293.7\) in Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests}).

As we've seen, estimate values in Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests} comprise the non-zero values in the resulting stand and stock tables. The zero values in a stand or stock table are just as important as the non-zero values because they denote the absence of a given species and DBH class combination and hence contribute to management decisions and forest valuation. These \emph{explicit} zeros in the stand and stock tables are \emph{implicit} zeros in Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests}.

Lastly, it's useful to add row and column totals to the stand and stock table margins. For Tables \ref{tab:stand1Stand} and \ref{tab:stand1Stock} the row totals are number of trees and volume per acre for each species, respectively. Similarly, column totals are the number of trees and volume per acre for each DBH class.

\begin{table}[!h]

\caption{\label{tab:stand1Stock}Stock table for estimates given in Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests}. Values are volume ft$^3$ per acre.}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{ccccc}
\toprule
\multicolumn{1}{c}{ } & \multicolumn{3}{c}{DBH classes (in)} & \multicolumn{1}{c}{ } \\
\cmidrule(l{3pt}r{3pt}){2-4}
Species & {}[6,10] & (10,14] & (14,18] & Totals\\
\midrule
Abies balsamea & 0.0 & 142.8 & 0.0 & 142.8\\
Betula papyrifera & 0.0 & 0.0 & 563.3 & 563.3\\
Pinus strobus & 116.3 & 375.5 & 0.0 & 491.8\\
\cline{2-5}
Totals & 116.3 & 518.3 & 563.3 & 1197.9\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Drawing on the preceding sections in this and previous \texttt{tidyverse} chapters, we show how a piped series of functions can efficiently deliver stand, stock, and similar tables with a fraction of the pain incurred using hand calculations or traditional spreadsheets.

Here are a few considerations for building stand and stock tables from forest inventory data:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Apply the appropriate \emph{estimator} to generate the desired values for the stand or stock table. These topics will be covered in Chapters \ref{statBasics}, \ref{forestStat}, and \ref{samplingDesigns}.
\item
  Identify the quantitative discrete and continuous variables of interest as well as the categorical variables you'll use to define the table's rows and columns.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Often, we need to convert a continuous variable into a categorical variable to represent the table's rows or columns. For example, if DBH was collected as a continuous variable (which is often the case), then it'll need to be \emph{discretized}, i.e., converted to a categorical variable, for use in a \texttt{dplyr} grouped summarize. The way in which you discretize your continuous variable depends on what information you want to communicate with the resulting table. For stand and stock tables, it's common to see continuous DBH measurements discretized to 2 or 4 inch DBH classes (or equivalent widths in centimeters). The \texttt{ggplot2} package \texttt{cut\_width()}, \texttt{cut\_number()}, and \texttt{cut\_interval()} functions provide convenient ways to discretize continuous variables.\\
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Reshape your data to long format using \texttt{tidyr} functions, if needed, so they can be easily piped into a series of \texttt{dplyr} functions used to form the desired stand or stock table.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    In practice you'll encounter a variety of different data collection, recording, and storage protocols that yield all sorts of different data organization formats that inevitably need wrangling into a format suitable to \texttt{dplyr}.
  \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

Our focus is on the second two considerations listed above. We'll work on the first consideration in subsequent chapters. For now, however, we begin with the table of estimates provided in Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests} and develop code to reproduce Tables \ref{tab:stand1Stand} and \ref{tab:stand1Stock}.\\
We begin by reading tree estimates from Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests} into the \texttt{ests} tibble.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ests \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"datasets/stand\_stock\_estimates.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Next, in the code below, we use \texttt{cut\_width()} to discretize \texttt{DBH\_in} and add this new discretized column, called \texttt{DBH\_4in}, back to \texttt{ests} via \texttt{mutate()}.\footnote{Run \texttt{cut\_width(DBH\_in,\ width\ =\ 4)} in the console, try different \texttt{width} argument values, and consult its manual page via \texttt{?ggplot2::cut\_width} to better understand the function's use and behavior.} Also in the \texttt{mutate()} call, the \texttt{.after} argument positions the new \texttt{DBH\_4in} column to the right of \texttt{DBH\_in} to facilitate visual comparison. \texttt{cut\_width()} (and related functions \texttt{cut\_number()} and \texttt{cut\_interval()}), provide a lot of control over how intervals are formed and how values on their endpoints are handled.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ests \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{ests }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{DBH\_4in =} \KeywordTok{cut\_width}\NormalTok{(DBH\_in, }\DataTypeTok{width =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), }\DataTypeTok{.after =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_in)}
\NormalTok{ests}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 6 x 5
#>   Species          DBH_in DBH_4in trees_ac vol_cu_ft_ac
#>   <chr>             <dbl> <fct>      <dbl>        <dbl>
#> 1 Abies balsamea     11.3 (10,14]        8         143.
#> 2 Betula papyrife~   14.8 (14,18]        8         270.
#> 3 Betula papyrife~   15.4 (14,18]        8         294.
#> 4 Pinus strobus       9.8 [6,10]         8         116.
#> 5 Pinus strobus      10.7 (10,14]        8         144.
#> 6 Pinus strobus      13.1 (10,14]        8         232.
\end{verbatim}

Take a look at the new \texttt{DBH\_4in} column in the \texttt{ests} tibble above. Notice its a factor data type and its values use parentheses and square bracket notation to define interval endpoints---the same notation used for column labels in the desired stand and stock tables. Also, confirm that values in \texttt{DBH\_in} map to the correct \texttt{DBH\_4in} interval.

Next, recall, explicit zeros in stand and stock tables are implicit zeros in Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests}. Let's add these implicit zero to \texttt{ests} (i.e., make the implicit explicit by adding the species and DBH class combinations with zero trees and volume). This is done using \texttt{tidyr::complete()}. This function adds all missing value combinations for a discrete variable set. The first argument in \texttt{complete()} is the data, followed by column names used to form the combinations, then the \texttt{fill} argument is given a list of column names and their associated desired explicit values. Our call to \texttt{complete()} below adds missing combinations of \texttt{Species} and \texttt{DBH\_4in} and sets explicit values of \texttt{trees\_ac} and \texttt{vol\_cu\_ft\_ac} zero. Notice, by not specifying an explicit value for \texttt{DBH\_in} in the \texttt{fill} argument's list, the \texttt{NA} default value is used.\footnote{The \texttt{NA} value for \texttt{DBH\_in} is okay, because this variable is not needed to build subsequent stand and stock tables.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ests \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{ests }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{complete}\NormalTok{(Species, DBH\_4in, }\DataTypeTok{fill =} \KeywordTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{trees\_ac =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{vol\_cu\_ft\_ac =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{ests}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 11 x 5
#>    Species         DBH_4in DBH_in trees_ac vol_cu_ft_ac
#>    <chr>           <fct>    <dbl>    <dbl>        <dbl>
#>  1 Abies balsamea  [6,10]    NA          0           0 
#>  2 Abies balsamea  (10,14]   11.3        8         143.
#>  3 Abies balsamea  (14,18]   NA          0           0 
#>  4 Betula papyrif~ [6,10]    NA          0           0 
#>  5 Betula papyrif~ (10,14]   NA          0           0 
#>  6 Betula papyrif~ (14,18]   14.8        8         270.
#>  7 Betula papyrif~ (14,18]   15.4        8         294.
#>  8 Pinus strobus   [6,10]     9.8        8         116.
#>  9 Pinus strobus   (10,14]   10.7        8         144.
#> 10 Pinus strobus   (10,14]   13.1        8         232.
#> 11 Pinus strobus   (14,18]   NA          0           0
\end{verbatim}

Next, sum values in \texttt{trees\_ac} and \texttt{vol\_cu\_ft\_ac} columns that have the same species and DBH class.\footnote{This step could be done before the call to \texttt{complete()} and yield the same result.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ests \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{ests }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(Species, DBH\_4in) }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{summarize}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{sum\_trees\_ac =} \KeywordTok{sum}\NormalTok{(trees\_ac),}
            \DataTypeTok{sum\_vol\_cu\_ft\_ac =} \KeywordTok{sum}\NormalTok{(vol\_cu\_ft\_ac),}
            \DataTypeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}\NormalTok{) }\CommentTok{\# Species grouping in the resulting}
                            \CommentTok{\# ests tibble is not needed.}
\NormalTok{ests}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 9 x 4
#>   Species         DBH_4in sum_trees_ac sum_vol_cu_ft_ac
#>   <chr>           <fct>          <dbl>            <dbl>
#> 1 Abies balsamea  [6,10]             0               0 
#> 2 Abies balsamea  (10,14]            8             143.
#> 3 Abies balsamea  (14,18]            0               0 
#> 4 Betula papyrif~ [6,10]             0               0 
#> 5 Betula papyrif~ (10,14]            0               0 
#> 6 Betula papyrif~ (14,18]           16             563.
#> 7 Pinus strobus   [6,10]             8             116.
#> 8 Pinus strobus   (10,14]           16             376.
#> 9 Pinus strobus   (14,18]            0               0
\end{verbatim}

Okay, now we're in business! The only thing left to do is reshape \texttt{ests} using \texttt{tidyr}'s \texttt{pivot\_wider()} function to put \texttt{Species} along the rows and \texttt{DBH\_2in} across the columns and decided which variable to set as the resulting table's value. This is done in the code below to make the stand table.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{stand \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{ests }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{select}\NormalTok{(Species, DBH\_4in, sum\_trees\_ac) }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{pivot\_wider}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{names\_from =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_4in, }\DataTypeTok{values\_from =}\NormalTok{ sum\_trees\_ac)}

\NormalTok{stand}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 3 x 4
#>   Species           `[6,10]` `(10,14]` `(14,18]`
#>   <chr>                <dbl>     <dbl>     <dbl>
#> 1 Abies balsamea           0         8         0
#> 2 Betula papyrifera        0         0        16
#> 3 Pinus strobus            8        16         0
\end{verbatim}

Notice in the code above, we \texttt{select()} only those columns used in the call to \texttt{pivot\_wider()}.\footnote{Try removing the call to \texttt{select()} before \texttt{pivot\_wider()} and see what happens.}

The final touch is to add row and column totals---the code for which is provided below with minimal explanation. As you'll see, adding these totals is a bit convoluted; however, each code component has been covered previously, so see if you can work through it and understand the steps.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Sum across rows.}
\NormalTok{stand \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{stand }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{Totals =} \KeywordTok{rowSums}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{across}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{where}\NormalTok{(is.numeric))))}

\CommentTok{\# Sum down columns.  }
\NormalTok{stand \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{stand }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{  }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{summarize}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{Species =} \StringTok{"Totals"}\NormalTok{, }\KeywordTok{across}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{:}\KeywordTok{last\_col}\NormalTok{(), sum)) }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{bind\_rows}\NormalTok{(stand, .) }\CommentTok{\# This is a little tricky. Notice the . places the}
                      \CommentTok{\# output from summarize() after stands in the}
                      \CommentTok{\# bind\_rows(stands, .), i.e., it binds the row of}
                      \CommentTok{\# totals to the bottom of the stand tibble.}
                      \CommentTok{\# Perhaps there\textquotesingle{}s a cleaner way of doing this.}
         
\NormalTok{stand}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 4 x 5
#>   Species           `[6,10]` `(10,14]` `(14,18]` Totals
#>   <chr>                <dbl>     <dbl>     <dbl>  <dbl>
#> 1 Abies balsamea           0         8         0      8
#> 2 Betula papyrifera        0         0        16     16
#> 3 Pinus strobus            8        16         0     24
#> 4 Totals                   8        24        16     48
\end{verbatim}

The \texttt{stand} tibble above is the stand Table \ref{tab:stand1Stock}. Creating the stock table follows the exact same steps up to the call to \texttt{pivot\_wider()}.

We took care to introduce the idea of implicit zeros in the forest inventory estimates and how \texttt{complete()} is used to make those implicit zeros explicit. In Chapter \ref{forestStat}, making implicit zeros explicit via the \texttt{complete()} function will again be important for implementing methods that generate estimates from forest inventory data.

When building stand and stock tables, specifying the optional \texttt{values\_fill} argument in \texttt{pivot\_wider()} allows you to skip the prior call to \texttt{complete()}. Like \texttt{complete()} and its \texttt{fill} argument, \texttt{pivot\_wider()}'s \texttt{values\_fill} argument allows you to specify the explicit value for missing stand and stock table row and column combinations.

The code below rebuilds the stand table in a single workflow starting with a fresh copy of \texttt{ests} and taking advantage of \texttt{pivot\_wider()}'s \texttt{values\_fill} argument, which removes the need for \texttt{complete()} (we comment out the call to \texttt{complete()} and add \texttt{values\_fill\ =\ 0} to \texttt{pivot\_wider()}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ests \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"datasets/stand\_stock\_estimates.csv"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{stand \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{ests }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{DBH\_4in =} \KeywordTok{cut\_width}\NormalTok{(DBH\_in, }\DataTypeTok{width =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\CommentTok{\# complete(Species, DBH\_4in, fill = list(trees\_ac = 0)) \%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(Species, DBH\_4in) }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{summarize}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{sum\_trees\_ac =} \KeywordTok{sum}\NormalTok{(trees\_ac), }\DataTypeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{pivot\_wider}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{names\_from =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_4in, }\DataTypeTok{values\_from =}\NormalTok{ sum\_trees\_ac,}
            \DataTypeTok{values\_fill =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) }\CommentTok{\# Added values\_fill argument.}

\CommentTok{\# Add row and column totals.}
\NormalTok{stand \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{stand }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{Totals =} \KeywordTok{rowSums}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{across}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{where}\NormalTok{(is.numeric))))}

\CommentTok{\# Sum down columns.  }
\NormalTok{stand \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{stand }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{  }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{summarize}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{Species =} \StringTok{"Totals"}\NormalTok{, }\KeywordTok{across}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{:}\KeywordTok{last\_col}\NormalTok{(), sum)) }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{bind\_rows}\NormalTok{(stand, .)}

\NormalTok{stand}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 4 x 5
#>   Species           `(10,14]` `(14,18]` `[6,10]` Totals
#>   <chr>                 <dbl>     <dbl>    <dbl>  <dbl>
#> 1 Abies balsamea            8         0        0      8
#> 2 Betula papyrifera         0        16        0     16
#> 3 Pinus strobus            16         0        8     24
#> 4 Totals                   24        16        8     48
\end{verbatim}

On your own, modify the code above to rebuild the stock table using \texttt{vol\_cu\_ft\_ac} estimates. Your resulting stock table should match values in Table \ref{stand1Stock}.

\hypertarget{summaryTidr}{%
\section{Summary}\label{summaryTidr}}

This chapter covered key functions in the \texttt{tidyr} package.\footnote{As with other programming topics introduced here, we provide only a partial tour and refer you to package manual pages and vignettes for more complete coverage, e.g., run \texttt{browseVignettes(package="tidyr")} on the console to see all \texttt{tidyr} vignettes.} These functions simplify moving between dataset formats and other common data wrangling and analysis tasks. The chapter opens with a definition of tidy data. Tidy data simplify analysis and conversion to other formats. Messy data are not wrong or bad and, as illustrated using the stand and stock tables in Section \ref{standStockTabs}, are often effective formats for conveying information.

Section \ref{tidyrReshape} introduced and illustrated \texttt{pivot\_longer()} and \texttt{pivot\_wider()} functions for moving between long and wide formats. Section \ref{tidyrSeparate} covered the \texttt{separate\_wider\_delim()} and \texttt{unite()} functions used to split and combine character columns. Over these two sections, a piped workflow comprising \texttt{dplyr} and \texttt{tidyr} functions was developed to make the wide and messy FACE dataset tidy.

Forest stand and stock tables are fundamental to making informed forest management decisions. Generating such tables is a routine task and hence having an efficient and flexible software workflow to create these tables is important. Such a workflow was introduced in Section \ref{standStockTabs}. An important workflow component was to make explicit the implicit zeros in the forest estimates via the \texttt{tidyr} \texttt{complete()} function \emph{or} by specifying \texttt{values\_fill\ =\ 0} in \texttt{pivot\_wider()}. The stand and stock table workflow will be extended in Chapter \ref{forestStat} with steps to generate estimates from forest inventory data.

\hypertarget{exercisesTidyr}{%
\section{Exercises}\label{exercisesTidyr}}

The exercises use the Elk County timber cruise dataset introduced in Section \ref{elk}. These data were collected on a 271 acre forested property in Elk County, Pennsylvania, to inform the owner's forest management plan.

In these exercises you'll develop stand and stock tables using per acre estimates for acceptable growing stock (AGS) sawtimber number of trees, basal area, and number of logs by species. Here, AGS is defined as a desirable species that contains at least one USFS Grade 3 log, now or in the future, and likely to live another 15 years.\footnote{See \citet{hanks1976} for USFS log grade specifications.} For each sawtimber tree, the forester recorded the number of 16 foot logs (minimum 1/2 log) to the nearest 1/2 log to a minimum top diameter of 10 inches or to a point where the tree no longer meets USFS Grade 3 specifications due to forks, crook, excessive sweep, defect, etc. Tree diameter was measured at 4.5 feet above the ground (DBH) on the uphill side of the tree. All trees 1.0 inch DBH and larger were recorded by two inch diameter classes as follows with DBH range and recorded class given in Table \ref{tab:elkDBHClass}.

\begin{table}[!h]

\caption{\label{tab:elkDBHClass}DBH (in) range and associated class recorded in the Elk County cruise.}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{cc}
\toprule
DBH range & DBH class\\
\midrule
1.0 – 2.9 & 2\\
3.0 – 4.9 & 4\\
5.0 – 6.9 & 6\\
7.0 – 8.9 & 8\\
9.0 – 10.9 & 10\\
11.0 – 12.9 & 12\\
etc. & etc.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Estimates used to build the subsequent tables are provided in ``datasets/elk\_spp\_dbh\_estimates.csv''. Columns in this file are as follows.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{Species} tree species common name.
\item
  \texttt{DBH\_2in} two inch DBH class (see Table \ref{tab:elkDBHClass} for class range and endpoints).
\item
  \texttt{Trees\_ac} estimated number of trees per acre.
\item
  \texttt{BA\_ac} estimated square feet of basal area per acre.
\item
  \texttt{Logs\_ac} estimated number of 16 foot logs per acre.
\end{enumerate}

This data file is set up similar to Table \ref{tab:stand1Ests}. Methods to generate these estimates from tree measurement data will be covered in Chapter \ref{forestStat}.

For most exercises, we provide the desired output and it's your job to write the \texttt{tidyverse} code to reproduce the output. Use the pipe \texttt{tidyverse} workflows to answer all questions.

\begin{exercise}
\protect\hypertarget{exr:tidyr6}{}\label{exr:tidyr6}Build a stand table with species along rows, DBH classes along columns (using \texttt{DBH\_2in\_range} created in Exercise \ref{exr:tidyr5}), and trees per acre as values. Round the stand table's values to two digits.

In general, rounding should always be done after all calculations are complete. An efficient way to round all numeric columns in your completed tibble is to pipe it into \texttt{mutate(across(where(is.numeric),\ function(x)\ round(x,\ digits\ =\ 3)))}. This call to \texttt{mutate()} applies an \emph{anonymous function}---a function without a name---defined as \texttt{function(x)\ round(x,\ digits\ =\ 3)} to each numeric column. Here, the anonymous function is the \texttt{base::round()} function with our desired number of digits set to 3. If you're happy with \texttt{round()}'s default \texttt{digits\ =\ 1}, then there's no need for the anonymous function and the call to \texttt{mutate()} is simply \texttt{mutate(across(where(is.numeric),\ round))}. Anonymous functions are only needed when you want to define your own function or specify non-default argument values in existing functions.

The stock table's first several columns are printed below and the entire table can be found in ``datasets/tidyr\_exr/tidyr\_elk\_ags\_stand\_table.tsv''. Notice your row and column totals should equal the \texttt{sum\_trees\_ac} values found in Exercises \ref{exr:tidyr3} and \ref{exr:tidyr3}, respectively.
\end{exercise}

\end{verbatim}

\begin{exercise}
\protect\hypertarget{exr:tidyr9}{}\label{exr:tidyr9}Take your wide messy stock table from Exercise \ref{exr:tidyr8}, remove the total row and column, then reformat it to be long and tidy. The piped workflow will likely include a call to \texttt{filter()} or \texttt{slice()}, \texttt{select()}, and \texttt{pivot\_longer()}. Set the \texttt{pivot\_longer()} functions \texttt{names\_to\ =\ "DBH\_2in\_range"} and \texttt{values\_to\ =\ "Logs\_ac}. Note, the resutling tibble will be like \texttt{elk\_ags} but with \texttt{Logs\_ac} summed within species and DBH class, and with explicit zero rows added for missing species and DBH class combinations. Given there are 9 species and 10 DBH classes, the resulting tibble should have 90 rows.
\end{exercise}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 90 x 3
#>    Species DBH_2in_range sum_logs_ac
#>    <chr>   <chr>               <dbl>
#>  1 Beech   [11,13)              1.33
#>  2 Beech   [13,15)              0.58
#>  3 Beech   [15,17)              0.45
#>  4 Beech   [19,21)              0.1 
#>  5 Beech   [17,19)              0   
#>  6 Beech   [21,23)              0   
#>  7 Beech   [23,25)              0   
#>  8 Beech   [27,29)              0   
#>  9 Beech   [29,31]              0   
#> 10 Beech   [25,27)              0   
#> # i 80 more rows
\end{verbatim}

As you'll see in the next chapter, this long and tidy format is used for most calls to \texttt{ggplot2} functions to make informative and beautiful graphics.

\hypertarget{ggplot2}{%
\chapter{\texorpdfstring{Creating graphics with \texttt{ggplot2}}{Creating graphics with ggplot2}}\label{ggplot2}}

Visualizing raw data and data summaries as graphics is key to EDA and communicating analysis results. This chapter introduces the \texttt{ggplot2} package used to efficiently create beautiful and informative graphics. Unlike R's \texttt{base} graphics and other graphics packages that focus on providing functions to produce specific types of graphics (e.g., scatter plot, histogram, line graph), \texttt{ggplot2} takes a more flexible approach by defining and applying a \emph{grammar} for constructing graphics.\footnote{In the current context, \emph{grammar} is best defined as the ``art of expressing relations of things in construction'' \citep{johnson1969grammatical} or as the ``fundamental principles or rules of an art or science'' \citep{OED}.}

Like other \texttt{tidyverse} packages, Hadley Wickham is the lead author and developer of \texttt{ggplot2}. Hadley based his grammar for \texttt{ggplot2} on earlier work in the book ``Grammar of Graphics'' \citep{wilkinson2005grammar} that lays out a grammar to describe the fundamental features that underlie all statistical graphics. The \texttt{gg} in \texttt{ggplot2} stands for Grammar of Graphics. \citet{Wickham2010JCGS} defines the grammar used by \texttt{ggplot2} and maps the grammar's components to those proposed in \citet{wilkinson2005grammar}.

The authoritative references for learning to use \texttt{ggplot2} is the book ``\texttt{ggplot2}: Elegant Graphics for Data Analysis'' \citep{WickhamGgplot2Book} and the extensive online reference.\footnote{A free online version of this book is available at \url{https://ggplot2-book.org} and the reference is found at \url{https://ggplot2.tidyverse.org}} The aim of this chapter is to provide a brief introduction to the grammar components and how they work together to construct a graphic. This introduction will be done mostly through worked examples. We borrow heavily from the first chapter of \citet{WickhamGgplot2Book} and refer you to this excellent book to further develop your understanding. Additional learning resources are provided in Section \ref{summaryGgplot2}.

\hypertarget{ggplotComponents}{%
\section{Grammar components}\label{ggplotComponents}}

\emph{Grammar}, according to \citet{johnson1969grammatical}, is the ``art of expressing relations of things in construction.'' In the \texttt{ggplot2} context, ``things'' are data or data summaries visualized using geometric objects (e.g., points, lines, bars). ``Expressing relations'' between different data values is done using the geometric object's aesthetic attributes (e.g., location, color, shape, size). The geometric objects are set within a 1- or 2-dimensional space defined using some coordinate system. In this way we are ``constructing'' a graphic to convey relationships within and across data or data summaries.

The construction proceeds in layers, where each each layer depicts one or more feature of the \emph{data} we wish to visualize. The grammar components include the following.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \emph{geometries} define the geometric objects seen in the graphic (e.g., points, lines, bars). Each geometry has a set of aesthetic attributes that map to data variable values. Different geometries can have different aesthetics.
\item
  \emph{mappings} connect the data's variables to aesthetic attributes of the geometric object (e.g., location, color, shape, size).
\item
  \emph{scales} translate a variable's values to visual representation (and vice versa). For example, scales assign a categorical variable's value to a color or shape, or a continuous variable's value to a location in the graphic's 1- or 2-dimensional space.
\item
  \emph{statistical transformations} (\emph{stats} for short) summarize the data's variables in some way. For example, if the desired graphic is a histogram of the number of trees by species, then stats summarize the input data to create the necessary tree count by species variables. Or stat might discretize a continuous variable's values so they can be visualized as ordered categories (e.g., creating DBH classes like we saw in Section \ref{standStockTabs}.
\item
  \emph{facets} display a common graphic across panels each based on data subsets defined by one or more discrete variables. These are useful for visualizing how a variable's values used to create the graphic differ across the discrete variable(s) values used to define the facets.
\item
  \emph{coordinates} define the graphic's coordinate system into which the geometric objects are placed. Typically this is a Cartesian coordinate system defined using a horizontal x-axis and a vertical y-axis, but there are other options, e.g., polar coordinates used for pie charts.
\item
  \emph{themes} control the non-data elements of the graphic, like the font size, text placement, legend orientation, and background color. While the defaults in \texttt{ggplot2} have been chosen with care, you may need to consult other references to create an attractive plot.
\end{itemize}

As we'll see in the next few sections, a graphic may comprise multiple layers, with each depicting different features of the data. Often data are shared among layers, but that is not always the case. The next section provides some worked examples to help you get used to \texttt{ggplot2} syntax.

\hypertarget{ggplotInitialLook}{%
\section{An initial look}\label{ggplotInitialLook}}

We motivate this initial \texttt{ggplot2} exploration using the FEF dataset introduced in Section \ref{fef}. Recall the FEF data were collected to create allometric equations that foresters could use to estimate tree component weight based on species and DBH. Here, we'll work with a simplified version of the FEF data provided in ``datasets/FEF\_trees\_simple.csv''. In this file, each row corresponds to a set of tree measurements and columns are as follows.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{Species} tree species scientific name.
\item
  \texttt{DBH\_cm} DBH in centimeters.
\item
  \texttt{Height\_m} total tree height in meters.
\item
  \texttt{Stem\_kg} dry weight of main stem in kilograms.
\item
  \texttt{Otherwoody\_kg} dry weight of top and branch tree components in kilograms.
\end{enumerate}

The code below loads the \texttt{readr} and \texttt{ggplot2} packages, reads the simplified FEF data into \texttt{fef}, and prints the first few rows and columns.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\KeywordTok{library}\NormalTok{(ggplot2)}

\NormalTok{fef \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"datasets/FEF\_trees\_simple.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{fef}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 88 x 5
#>    Species     DBH_cm Height_m Stem_kg Otherwoody_kg
#>    <chr>        <dbl>    <dbl>   <dbl>         <dbl>
#>  1 Acer rubrum  15.2     14.6     54.7          50.4
#>  2 Acer rubrum  17.5     14.6     62.3          60.8
#>  3 Acer rubrum  16.3     14.6     73.3          41.2
#>  4 Acer rubrum  16.5     14.9     53.6          52.3
#>  5 Acer rubrum  18.3     15.5    106.           52.5
#>  6 Acer rubrum   7.87    12.2     11.7           2.5
#>  7 Acer rubrum   5.08     9.30     3.2           2  
#>  8 Acer rubrum  10.4     15.2     28.3          10.1
#>  9 Acer rubrum   6.10     8.53     5.5           2.5
#> 10 Acer rubrum   6.86    12.3     10.6           4.5
#> # i 78 more rows
\end{verbatim}

The next several sections provide an applied look at grammar component's role in constructing a basic graphic.

\hypertarget{geometries-mapping-and-aesthetics}{%
\subsection{Geometries, mapping, and aesthetics}\label{geometries-mapping-and-aesthetics}}

Scatter plots are a workhorse of data visualization and provide a good entry point to learning \texttt{ggplot2}. Let's begin by making a scatter plot of DBH versus stem weight using the \texttt{fef} data.

Code for all \texttt{ggplot2} graphics starts with \texttt{ggplot()} followed by layers and modifying functions added with the \texttt{+} operator. Use of the \texttt{+} operator underscores the constructive nature of \texttt{ggplot2} graphics.

The code below includes a call to \texttt{ggplot()} with the \texttt{data} argument set to \texttt{fef} and the \texttt{mapping} argument set to \texttt{aes()}, which is used to connect variables with anticipated geometry's aesthetics.\footnote{See aesthetics manual page \texttt{?ggplot2::aes}.} Here, aesthetic \texttt{x} is mapped to \texttt{DBH\_cm} and aesthetic \texttt{y} is mapped to \texttt{Stem\_kg}. By itself, the call to \texttt{ggplot()} doesn't define the type of graphical display to draw; hence, the resulting graphic is empty except for the axes defined by the \texttt{x} and \texttt{y} aesthetics. The scales grammar component is at work here applying defaults to set axes extents and value breaks, and determining many other visual features.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-285-1} \end{center}

To draw objects in the graph plane we need to specify a geometry that will do something with the supplied \texttt{x} and \texttt{y} aesthetics.\footnote{Take a look under the Geoms section in \url{https://ggplot2.tidyverse.org/reference} to get a sense of the verity of geometries that can be drawn.} The desired scatter plot has points drawn at \texttt{x} and \texttt{y} value pairs. Point geometries are drawn using \texttt{geom\_point()}. The code below adds (using the \texttt{+} operator) the point geometry.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-286-1} \end{center}

Looking at the \texttt{geom\_point()} manual page, you'll see the first two arguments are \texttt{mapping} and \texttt{data}. By default the values for these arguments are inherited from the initial call to \texttt{ggplot()}.\footnote{Access the manual page via \texttt{?ggplot2::geom\_point()}.} So, alternatively, values for \texttt{mapping} (and even \texttt{data}) can be specified in \texttt{geom\_point()}, and similarly for other geometries. The code below produces the same output as above, but specifies mapping within \texttt{geom\_point()}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg)) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-287-1} \end{center}

Return again to the \texttt{geom\_point()} manual page and scroll down to the Aesthetics section. This section lists all the \texttt{aes()} arguments used to map variables to point geometry features. Notice \texttt{x} and \texttt{y} are required, but all other aesthetics are optional.

Let's map the point color to \texttt{Species}. To do so, we include \texttt{color\ =\ Species} in the point geometry's \texttt{aes()} in the code below. Notice, in the resulting graphic a color scale for species and associated legend is automatically generated. Species is a categorical variable, hence scales selects a default discrete color scale. These, often very reasonable, default colors and legend placement decisions facilitate efficient exploration of data. Later, we'll show how to override these and other defaults.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-288-1} \end{center}

Recall from Section \ref{factors} discrete variables (e.g., integers or characters) can be represented as a factor. If you plan to represent integer or character variables as categories in your graphic, then it's often useful to convert them to factors before sending them into \texttt{ggplot()}. One particularly motivating reason to convert your discrete variables to factors is that it gives you finer control over how the categories are ordered and labeled in the resulting legends and other graphic elements. As you'll notice in the graphic above, the default is to list species in alphabetical order within the legend. Say, for some reason, we'd like them in reverse alphabetical order. This can be done by converting \texttt{Species} to a factor and specifying the factor levels in the order we wish for them to be displayed in the legend.\footnote{In Section \ref{ggplotScales}, we show an alternative and, in some situations, simpler approach that uses scales to control discrete variable ordering in the graphic's elements.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{fef \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{fef }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{Species =} \KeywordTok{factor}\NormalTok{(Species, }
                          \DataTypeTok{levels =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Prunus serotina"}\NormalTok{, }
                                     \StringTok{"Liriodendron tulipifera"}\NormalTok{,}
                                     \StringTok{"Betula lenta"}\NormalTok{,}
                                     \StringTok{"Acer rubrum"}\NormalTok{)))}

\NormalTok{fef }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{glimpse}\NormalTok{() }\CommentTok{\# Check out the Species factor.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> Rows: 88
#> Columns: 5
#> $ Species       <fct> Acer rubrum, Acer rubrum, Acer ~
#> $ DBH_cm        <dbl> 15.240, 17.526, 16.256, 16.510,~
#> $ Height_m      <dbl> 14.6304, 14.6304, 14.6304, 14.9~
#> $ Stem_kg       <dbl> 54.7, 62.3, 73.3, 53.6, 106.4, ~
#> $ Otherwoody_kg <dbl> 50.4, 60.8, 41.2, 52.3, 52.5, 2~
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-290-1} \end{center}

Next, let's map color to the continuous \texttt{Height\_m} variable by setting \texttt{color\ =\ Height\_m} in the point geometry's \texttt{aes()}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Height\_m)) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-291-1} \end{center}

As you can see by the resulting legend colorbar, when the color aesthetic is mapped to a continuous variable, e.g., \texttt{Height\_m}, scales assign each possible value in the variable's range to a color along a color gradient.\footnote{See what happens when you force \texttt{Height\_m} to be treated as a discrete variable, i.e.~set \texttt{color\ =\ as.factor(Height\_m)}.} As expected, the graphic above shows that taller trees have larger DBH and stem weight.

As you've seen in the \texttt{geom\_point()} manual page, there are many possible aesthetics to which we can map a variable. The aesthetic's defaults are set up to handle ``reasonable'' mappings. Here reasonable means that scales can make an interpretable mapping between the variable's type (i.e., categorical or continuous) and aesthetic. Above we mapped species (categorical) to color and height (continuous) to color, both resulted in reasonable mappings. Mapping species or height to the size aesthetic will also work by default.\footnote{Although representing species with point size is not very meaningful.} The shape aesthetic is well suited for species, but it's not immediately apparent how to map a shape to height as suggested by the error thrown by the code below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{shape =}\NormalTok{ Height\_m)) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> Error in `geom_point()`:
#> ! Problem while computing aesthetics.
#> i Error occurred in the 1st layer.
#> Caused by error in `scale_f()`:
#> ! A continuous variable cannot be mapped to the shape aesthetic
#> i choose a different aesthetic or use `scale_shape_binned()`
\end{verbatim}

In addition to being reasonable, the geometry must also be able to display the aesthetic. For example, the fill aesthetic is only applicable for \texttt{geom\_point()} if the point shape has a fill aesthetic.\footnote{See \texttt{vignette("ggplot2-specs")} to learn about each geometry's aesthetics, e.g., find the Point section in the vignette and read about the various values for \texttt{shape} and how \texttt{color}, \texttt{fill}, and \texttt{stroke} may, or may not, apply to each shape.} The code below changes the default point shape to 21 (which has color, fill, and stroke aesthetic), we map fill to \texttt{Height\_m} and set values for size, color, and stroke to be the same for all points. The key thing to notice here is size, color, and stroke aesthetics are specified outside of \texttt{aes()} and, hence, not mapped to a data variable name.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{fill =}\NormalTok{ Height\_m), }
             \DataTypeTok{shape =} \DecValTok{21}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{color =} \StringTok{"black"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{stroke =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-293-1} \end{center}

Next let's map height to color and species to shape. Note we also change the size of the points to a static value.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Height\_m, }
                           \DataTypeTok{shape =}\NormalTok{ Species), }\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-294-1} \end{center}

While the figure above compactly communicates several different relationships---via color and shape scales---consideration should always be given to the audience. It takes time and thought to creating clear and concise graphics that efficiently communicate information. Always keep your audience in mind. What are the key points you want to communicate and are those points clearly portrayed in the graphic? How hard does the audience have to work to understand what you're trying to communicate? Would multiple graphics more clearly communicate key information? In some cases, portraying multiple scales in a single graphic is the perfect way to communicate information, in other cases it might be preferable to offer scale specific graphics.

The \texttt{ggplot2} package is a tool for a broad range of tasks. At one extreme, the package offers functionality to produce publication quality sophisticated and beautiful graphics, at the other extreme it's a tool for rapid data exploration used to identify errors, features, patterns, and possible avenues for analysis. In the latter extreme, there's an audience of one---yourself---and the focus is on efficient data exploration using as little code as possible.

You can create new variables directly within \texttt{aes()}. For example, say we wanted to compare \emph{Prunus serotina} to other species in the scatter. Math and logical operators are evaluated on variables in \texttt{aes()} prior to assigning them to the aesthetic. This is illustrated in the code below, where the logical \texttt{Species\ ==\ "Prunus\ serotina"} result is assigned to \texttt{color} and a discrete scale is rendered.\footnote{Try this with a continues variable, e.g., identify all trees over 15 meters using \texttt{aes(x\ =\ DBH\_cm,\ y\ =\ Stem\_kg,\ color\ =\ Height\_m\ \textgreater{}\ 15)}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }
                           \DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species }\OperatorTok{==}\StringTok{ "Prunus serotina"}\NormalTok{),}
             \DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-295-1} \end{center}

The scatter plot above makes it instantly clear that \emph{Prunus serotina} was sampled fairly evenly across the DBH range and other species were not sampled beyond about 20 cm DBH and 125 kg stem weight. Notice the efficiency of creating new variables within \texttt{aes()} compared with creating a new variable and adding it to \texttt{fef} via \texttt{mutate()} prior to calling \texttt{ggplot()}. The ability to create new variables within \texttt{aes()} on-the-fly and with good defaults for a graphic's numerous visual features is key to efficient EDA.

Additional geometry layers are added with the \texttt{+} operator and inherit data and aesthetic mappings by default. For example, let's add a smooth line geometry layer over the points. The code below uses the \texttt{geom\_smooth()} function to add the desired line. There are a lot of steps going on behind the scenes here. For example, the \texttt{stats} package is fitting a statistical model to the \texttt{x} and \texttt{y} mappings to estimate the smooth trend line with an associated measure of uncertainty. Here, the trend line appears in a default color blue and the confidence interval band in gray.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-296-1} \end{center}

Notice the smooth line above is layered over the points. This layer order is because \texttt{geom\_smooth()} is added after \texttt{geom\_point()}---simply switch the layer order if you'd like the points over the line.

The code below places the smooth line beneath the points, adds the species \texttt{color} aesthetic, and removes the confidence interval band by setting the \texttt{geom\_smooth()} \texttt{se} argument to \texttt{FALSE}. Notice, the \texttt{color} aesthetic is now applied to both the point and line geometries such that a separate smooth line is produced for each species.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef,}
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{se =} \OtherTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-297-1} \end{center}

\hypertarget{ggplotScales}{%
\subsection{Scales}\label{ggplotScales}}

Let's work more directly with scales. Scales translate a variable's values to visual representation (and vice versa). In the previous example, we had \texttt{mapping\ =\ aes(x\ =\ DBH\_cm,\ y\ =\ Stem\_kg,\ color\ =\ Species)}, which suggests we could use scales to modify the default representation for \texttt{x}, \texttt{y}, and \texttt{color} aesthetics.

Most functions that modify scales are of the form \texttt{scale\_*()} where the asterisk is the name of the scale and, usually, some additional description of the scale type or how you intend for it to be modified.\footnote{Take a look in the Scales section in \url{https://ggplot2.tidyverse.org/reference} to get a sense of the functions used to modify aesthetic scales.}

Let's begin with changing color scale defaults. Creating and applying color palettes to maximize visual appeal, accessibility, and informative transfer is a science in of itself (see, e.g., references on color theory). Broadly there are three types of color palettes used for data visualization.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Qualitative -- used for discrete unordered numeric or categorical variables. Discrete values are paired with a color and all colors receive the same interpreted weight. Colors are typically chosen to be distinct from one another to emphasize the lack of natural ordering and improve visual differentiation among values.
\item
  Sequential -- used for continuous numeric, or discrete ordered numeric or categorical variables. Continuous numeric values are represented with a color gradient depicting a continuum from high to low or low to high. Similarly, discrete ordered values are paired with colors in a color gradient, with colors chosen to depict the incremental value change from one discrete value to the next.
\item
  Divergent -- used for continuous numeric, or discrete ordered numeric or categorical variables that increase and decrease from a neutral or central value. Values are represented using two diverging color gradients. The gradients start at a specified central value and extend to two extremes.
\end{itemize}

Species is an unordered categorical variable. When \texttt{color\ =\ Species} the default color scale is implicitly set with \texttt{scale\_color\_discrete()}.\footnote{In general, there is an equivalent \texttt{scale\_fill\_*()} for every \texttt{scale\_color\_*()}.
