If you map a variable to the \texttt{fill} aesthetic use \texttt{scale\_fill\_discrete()} to modify its defaults.} We make the call to \texttt{scale\_color\_discrete()} explicit below, again the defaults are used when no arguments are specified.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef,}
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_color\_discrete}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-298-1} \end{center}

As illustrated in the code and output above, defaults are used if no arguments are passed into the scale function. Let's take a look at the \texttt{scale\_color\_discrete()} manual page, via \texttt{?ggplot2::scale\_color\_hue}, to get a sense of defaults that can be changed.\footnote{Note when you go to the \texttt{scale\_color\_discrete()} manual page, via \texttt{?ggplot2::scale\_color\_discrete}, you learn that \texttt{scale\_color\_discrete()} is actually calling \texttt{scale\_color\_hue()} hence the reason we point you to the \texttt{scale\_color\_hue()} manual page.} Scroll down in the manual page and read about the \texttt{name} argument. All scale functions have a \texttt{name} argument and it's used to provide a better title for the given aesthetic in the graphic. The default for \texttt{name} is the aesthetic's variable name, e.g., the variable name mapped to \texttt{color} is used as the color legend title and variables mapped to \texttt{x} and \texttt{y} are used for the respective axis title (you can see this in the graphic above where \texttt{x} and \texttt{y} titles are DBH\_cm and Stem\_kg, respectively, and the color legend title is Species). The code below changes the color scale name from its default to ``Scientific name''.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef,}
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_color\_discrete}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{name =} \StringTok{"Scientific name"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-299-1} \end{center}

Next let's modify the order that species appear in the legend. We saw earlier that order can be changed through the factor data type specification, but it can also be changed using the scale function's \texttt{limits} argument. Returning to the manual page, notice an optional value for \texttt{limits} is ``a character vector that defines possible values of the scale and their order.'' We specify our desired species order below in the vector assigned to \texttt{limits}. For fun, we also remove the legend title by setting \texttt{name} to \texttt{NULL} (a title is not really needed here, species names and associated colors make the legend's role self-evident).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef,}
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_color\_discrete}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{name =} \OtherTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
                       \DataTypeTok{limits =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Acer rubrum"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Betula lenta"}\NormalTok{, }
                                  \StringTok{"Liriodendron tulipifera"}\NormalTok{, }
                                  \StringTok{"Prunus serotina"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-300-1} \end{center}

There are specialized scale functions available to simplify changing defaults and provide expanded options for creating interesting and informative features. For example, in the code below, \texttt{scale\_color\_discrete()} is replaced with \texttt{scale\_color\_manual()} that is used to set a qualitative color palette we defined in \texttt{spp\_col}.\footnote{Recall, you can see R's built-in colors by running \texttt{colors()} on the console.} Our chosen color pallete is passed to \texttt{scale\_color\_manual()} via the \texttt{values} argument. Additionally, we again use the scale's \texttt{name} argument to change the default title ``Species'' to ``Scientific name''.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{spp\_col \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Acer rubrum"}\NormalTok{ =}\StringTok{ "firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Betula lenta"}\NormalTok{ =}\StringTok{ "plum"}\NormalTok{, }
             \StringTok{"Liriodendron tulipifera"}\NormalTok{ =}\StringTok{ "aquamarine"}\NormalTok{, }
             \StringTok{"Prunus serotina"}\NormalTok{ =}\StringTok{ "steelblue"}\NormalTok{)}

\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef,}
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_color\_manual}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{name =} \StringTok{"Scientific name"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{values =}\NormalTok{ spp\_col)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-301-1} \end{center}

These colors are pretty nice, however, we might be better off leaving color palette selection to the experts. There are several functions within \texttt{ggplot2} that provide thoughtfully designed color palettes. The code below uses \texttt{scale\_color\_brewer()} to provide palettes selected by Cynthia Brewer, a highly accomplished cartographer well known for her ColorBrewer palettes.\footnote{See \url{https://colorbrewer2.org} for a visual tour of ColorBrewer palettes. The web application allows for visualization of different palettes and indicates which are colorblind-friendly and print well in black and white.} The \texttt{palette} argument within \texttt{scale\_color\_brewer()} accepts a palette name. For example, we selected the Set2 palette in the code below\footnote{See \texttt{?ggplot2::scale\_color\_brewer} for the list of palette names.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef,}
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_color\_brewer}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{name =} \StringTok{"Latin name"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{palette =} \StringTok{"Set2"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-302-1} \end{center}

There are lots of exciting color palettes within \texttt{ggplot2} and available as R packages created by color enthusiasts. For example, we enjoy using palettes from the American filmmaker Wes Anderson's movies via the \texttt{wesanderson} package illustrated below.\footnote{You will need to install the \texttt{wesanderson} package by running \texttt{install.packages("wesanderson")} in your console before calling \texttt{library(wesanderson)}.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{library}\NormalTok{(wesanderson)}

\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef,}
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{se =} \OtherTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_color\_manual}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{name =} \StringTok{"Latin name"}\NormalTok{, }
                     \DataTypeTok{values =} \KeywordTok{wes\_palette}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"AsteroidCity1"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-303-1} \end{center}

When a color aesthetic is mapped to a continuous variable, the variable's values are associated with colors in a sequential or divergent gradient. We saw such a mapping previously when \texttt{color\ =\ Height\_m} and the default colorbar gradient went from dark blue (small height values) to light blue (large height values).

We can select different built-in color gradients or create our own using a \texttt{scale\_*()} function. For example, there are several excellent built-in continuous and divergent colorbrewer palletes to choose from via \texttt{scale\_color\_brewer()} or \texttt{scale\_fill\_brewer()}. Alternately, create your own using \texttt{scale\_*\_gradient()} for a two color gradient (low-high), \texttt{scale\_*\_gradient2()} for a diverging color gradient (low-mid-high), or \texttt{scale\_*\_gradientn()} for an n-color gradient, where \texttt{*} is either \texttt{color} or \texttt{fill}.

The example code below uses \texttt{scale\_fill\_gradient()} to create a two color gradient from hex color \#E28A00 to \#0201A5.\footnote{A color hex code is a combination of alphanumeric characters beginning with the pound symbol that uniquely represent a color's unique combination of red, green, and blue. It is the most common way to digitally represent colors.} Like many R functions, colors can be specified as quoted R color names or hex colors.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef,}
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{fill =}\NormalTok{ Height\_m)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{shape =} \DecValTok{21}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{color =} \StringTok{"black"}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_fill\_gradient}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{low =} \StringTok{"\#E28A00"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{high =} \StringTok{"\#0201A5"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-304-1} \end{center}

Just for fun, we'll try the \texttt{scale\_fill\_gradientn()} function that builds a color gradient using any number of colors. The code below generates 10 colors from the \texttt{wesanderson} package Zissou1 palette. In the code, we also change the colorbar name to ``Height (m)'' and add breaks at 7, 13.5, and 20 cm.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg,}\DataTypeTok{fill =}\NormalTok{ Height\_m)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{shape =} \DecValTok{21}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{color =} \StringTok{"black"}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_fill\_gradientn}\NormalTok{(}
    \DataTypeTok{name =} \StringTok{"Height (m)"}\NormalTok{,}
    \DataTypeTok{breaks =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{13.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{),}
    \DataTypeTok{colors =} \KeywordTok{wes\_palette}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{name =} \StringTok{"Zissou1"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{n =} \DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{type =} \StringTok{"continuous"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-305-1} \end{center}

Changes to default scale behaviors for \texttt{x} and \texttt{y} aesthetics are also controlled with scale functions. Like color scales, the underlying data type determines the scale function. An axis is either continuous or discrete, where discrete subsumes both ordered and unordered categorical data types. For our scatter plot, both \texttt{DBH\_cm} (\texttt{x}) and \texttt{Stem\_kg} (\texttt{y}) are continuous, and can be modified via the \texttt{scale\_x\_continuous()} and \texttt{scale\_y\_continuous()} functions, respectively. Take a moment to look at the manual pages for these functions. The functions allow you to change the axis name, tick mark break number and location, limits, and many other defaults.

The code below uses \texttt{x} and \texttt{y} scale functions to display a more descriptive name, force an origin at zero, increase the maximum limit, and add more breaks. For illustration, x-axis breaks were provided explicitly via the \texttt{breaks} argument and the suggested number of y-axis breaks via the \texttt{n.breaks} argument. The \texttt{limits} argument takes a vector of length two that specifies the minimum and maximum axis value. The default plot includes a bit of padding on the axis ends to ensure data are positioned away from the axis limits. You can change this default behavior using the \texttt{expand} argument and can use the \texttt{expansion()} function to generate values to give to \texttt{expand}. We specify zero multiplicative and additive padding at the axis lower limit (i.e., the origin) and default padding at the axis upper limit (see \texttt{?ggplot2::expansion}).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef,}
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_x\_continuous}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{name =} \StringTok{"DBH (cm)"}\NormalTok{, }
                     \DataTypeTok{breaks =} \KeywordTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{26}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\DataTypeTok{limits=}\KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{26}\NormalTok{), }
                     \DataTypeTok{expand =} \KeywordTok{expansion}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{mult =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{), }
                                        \DataTypeTok{add =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{))) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_y\_continuous}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{name =} \StringTok{"Stem weight (kg)"}\NormalTok{,  }
                     \DataTypeTok{n.breaks =} \DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{limits =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{160}\NormalTok{), }
                     \DataTypeTok{expand =} \KeywordTok{expansion}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{mult =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{), }
                                        \DataTypeTok{add =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-306-1} \end{center}

The \texttt{xlim()} and \texttt{ylim()} functions provide a shortcut to specifying axis limits (e.g., we could use \texttt{xlim(0,\ 26)} in place of \texttt{scale\_x\_continuous(limits=c(0,\ 26))}). More generally, the \texttt{lims()} function is a shortcut to adjusting limits for any aesthetic. Similarly, shortcuts are provided for axis scale names via \texttt{xlab()} and \texttt{ylab()}, and a general function \texttt{labs()} used for any aesthetic. The code below uses \texttt{labs()} to change axis and color names. The \texttt{labs()} function can also be used to add descriptive text to other parts of the graphic such as a title, subtitle, and caption as illustrated below.\footnote{The \texttt{ggtitle()} function can also be used to add title, subtitle, and caption.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef,}
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =} \StringTok{"DBH (cm)"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Stem weight (kg)"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Latin name"}\NormalTok{,}
       \DataTypeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Relationship between DBH and stem weight by species"}\NormalTok{,}
       \DataTypeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"USDA Fernow Experimental Forest"}\NormalTok{,}
       \DataTypeTok{caption =} \StringTok{"Data source: www.fs.usda.gov"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-307-1} \end{center}

We often want to zoom into a region of a graphic to highlight a feature or get a better look at a relationship. There are two approaches to zooming.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Adjust axis scale limits to the data range of interest. This approach, however, can have unintended consequences and is not recommended. The unintended consequences occur because data outside an axis scale limit is removed from the data used to create the graphic, i.e., stats are computed using the visual data subset.
\item
  Set limits on the coordinate system. Setting limits on the coordinate system will zoom into the defined region (like you're looking at it with a magnifying glass), and will not change the underlying data. In our current example, we're using the Cartesian coordinate system, limits of which are set using \texttt{coord\_cartesian()}.
\end{enumerate}

The next two bits of code and output illustrate the zooming approaches and the unintended consequences of setting axis scale limits.

The smooth line in the graphic below shows the relationship between DBH and stem weight (along with an associated confidence interval) using the entire \texttt{fef} dataset.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-308-1} \end{center}

Say we want to get a better look at the relationship for trees with DBH between 18 and 24 cm. Using the first approach, we adjust axis scale limits using \texttt{xlim()} (we could have also used the \texttt{limits} argument in \texttt{scale\_x\_continuous()}). As the warning below the code suggests, this approach removes the 74 \texttt{fef} rows. These removed rows are trees with \texttt{DBH\_cm} outside the 18 to 24 cm range.\footnote{In fact all 74 trees have DBH less than 18 cm, which you can prove to yourself via \texttt{sum(fef\$DBH\_cm\ \textless{}\ 18)} or \texttt{fef\ \%\textgreater{}\%\ filter(DBH\_cm\ \textless{}\ 18)\ \%\textgreater{}\%\ nrow()}} Hence, the smooth line is fit to only those trees within the limits and the result looks very different from the relationship of interest in the graphic above.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{xlim}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{18}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{24}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> Warning: Removed 74 rows containing non-finite values
#> (`stat_smooth()`).
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
#> Warning: Removed 74 rows containing missing values
#> (`geom_point()`).
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-309-1} \end{center}

The code below uses the second and preferred approach. This is simply a zoom in on the 18 to 24 cm region of the graphic and uses the full \texttt{fef} dataset to estimate the smooth line and its confidence interval.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{coord\_cartesian}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{xlim =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{18}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{24}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-310-1} \end{center}

\hypertarget{ggplotGuidesThemes}{%
\subsection{Guides and Themes}\label{ggplotGuidesThemes}}

There are many other features that should be considered when making visually appealing, informative, and accessible graphics. These include legend layout, size, placement, orientation, and font type, size, placement, and the graphic's general look and feel. Fine tuning scale defaults is accomplished via scale \emph{guides}, whereas manipulating the graphic's long list of non-data elements is done via themes.

A scale's \texttt{guide} argument requires a guide object created using a guide function such as \texttt{guide\_axis()}, \texttt{guide\_legend()}, or \texttt{guide\_colorbar()}. These and similar \texttt{guide\_*()} functions can be specified in their respective scale functions or more generally for any scale within \texttt{guides()}. We most frequently use \texttt{guide\_legend()}, \texttt{guide\_colorbar()}, and similar legend type guides. For example let's make the colorbar a bit taller and narrower via the \texttt{barheight} and \texttt{barwidth} arguments. See \texttt{?ggplot2::guide\_colorbar()} for arguments to make these and many other changes.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Height\_m)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_color\_continuous}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{name =} \StringTok{"Height (m)"}\NormalTok{,}
                         \DataTypeTok{guide =} \KeywordTok{guide\_colorbar}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{barheight =} \DecValTok{15}\NormalTok{, }
                                                \DataTypeTok{barwidth =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-311-1} \end{center}

Arguments passed to the \texttt{theme()} function control a wide array of non-data graphic elements and is where we spend much of our time getting the graphic to look ``just right.'' Take a look at \texttt{?ggplot2::theme} to get a sense of the defaults that you can change. There are also several readymade complete themes designed to set a suite of theme defaults, see \texttt{?ggplot2::theme\_gray}. We typically choose one of these complete themes then further modify it's defaults via \texttt{theme()}.

In the following example, we aim for a fairly polished looking scatter plot by adding useful labels to the scales and choosing the \texttt{theme\_bw()} complete theme, which creates a classic black-on-white look, then change a long list of the theme's defaults. As you'll see, many \texttt{theme()} arguments require an object returned by a function, e.g., the \texttt{text} argument needs an object returned by the \texttt{element\_text()} function (see \texttt{?ggplot::element\_text} for ways text can be modified).\footnote{Also see the \texttt{base\_size} argument in any \texttt{theme\_*()} function to quickly manipulate default text size of a graphic.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Height\_m)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Height (m)"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{x =} \StringTok{"DBH (cm)"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Stem weight (kg)"}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{theme\_bw}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{text =} \KeywordTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{), }\CommentTok{\# Increase all text elements.}
        \DataTypeTok{axis.text =} \KeywordTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{), }\CommentTok{\# Increase axis tick labels.}
        \DataTypeTok{legend.text =} \KeywordTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{), }\CommentTok{\# Increase legend labels.}
        \DataTypeTok{panel.grid.minor =} \KeywordTok{element\_blank}\NormalTok{(), }\CommentTok{\# Remove minor grid lines.}
        \DataTypeTok{legend.direction =} \StringTok{"horizontal"}\NormalTok{, }\CommentTok{\# Make the colorbar horizontal.}
        \DataTypeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"bottom"} \CommentTok{\# Move the colorbar to the bottom.}
\NormalTok{        ) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-312-1} \end{center}

This looks pretty good. It might look better to move the colorbar title from the left to the top center of the legend. Changing the location of the colorbar title and centering adjustments are done using the color scale guide as illustrated below (same code as above but with \texttt{scale\_color\_continuous()} added).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Height\_m)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Height (m)"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{x =} \StringTok{"DBH (cm)"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Stem weight (kg)"}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{theme\_bw}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{text =} \KeywordTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{),}
        \DataTypeTok{axis.text =} \KeywordTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{),}
        \DataTypeTok{legend.text =} \KeywordTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{),}
        \DataTypeTok{panel.grid.minor =} \KeywordTok{element\_blank}\NormalTok{(),}
        \DataTypeTok{legend.direction =} \StringTok{"horizontal"}\NormalTok{,}
        \DataTypeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"bottom"}
\NormalTok{        ) }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_color\_continuous}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{guide =} \KeywordTok{guide\_colorbar}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{title.position =} \StringTok{"top"}\NormalTok{,}
                                                \DataTypeTok{title.hjust =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-313-1} \end{center}

As you learn \texttt{ggplot2}, figuring out which graphic components to modify to achieve your desired change can be challenging (and perhaps a bit frustrating). However, Google, user forums, manual pages, and the ever expanding set of \texttt{ggplot2} references and vignettes will be key to developing proficiency and a codebase upon which you can build graphics for future projects.

\hypertarget{ggplotFacets}{%
\subsection{Facets}\label{ggplotFacets}}

Figure \ref{fig:FEFPlot}, in Section \ref{fef}, shows the relationship between DBH and component weight for four different tree components and four different species. Each of the 16 panels that comprise this graphic show a unique component and species combination. A facet is one side of something with many sides, hence the naming for \texttt{facet\_*()} functions that create these multi-panel graphics. The \texttt{facet\_grid()} function forms a matrix of panels defined by row and column faceting variables. It's most useful when you have two discrete variables, and all combinations of the variables exist in the data. The \texttt{facet\_wrap()} function is useful if you have one variable with multiple levels.

Creating a facet graphic is as easy as adding \texttt{facet\_grid()} or \texttt{facet\_wrap()} to your \texttt{ggplot()} expression and identifying the faceting variable or variables using the \texttt{vars()} function, as illustrated below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{facet\_wrap}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{vars}\NormalTok{(Species))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-314-1} \end{center}

As you can see above, the facet default is to share axes scales across all panels. You can allow each panel to have its own scales computed from its data subset by adding \texttt{scales\ =\ "free"} (or values \texttt{"free\_x"} or \texttt{"free\_y"} for axis specific control), as illustrated below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{facet\_wrap}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{vars}\NormalTok{(Species), }\DataTypeTok{scales =} \StringTok{"free"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-315-1} \end{center}

The \texttt{fef} data subset we're working with includes weight measurements for stem (\texttt{Stem\_kg}) and other woody material (\texttt{Otherwood\_kg}) components (see below).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{fef}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 88 x 5
#>    Species     DBH_cm Height_m Stem_kg Otherwoody_kg
#>    <fct>        <dbl>    <dbl>   <dbl>         <dbl>
#>  1 Acer rubrum  15.2     14.6     54.7          50.4
#>  2 Acer rubrum  17.5     14.6     62.3          60.8
#>  3 Acer rubrum  16.3     14.6     73.3          41.2
#>  4 Acer rubrum  16.5     14.9     53.6          52.3
#>  5 Acer rubrum  18.3     15.5    106.           52.5
#>  6 Acer rubrum   7.87    12.2     11.7           2.5
#>  7 Acer rubrum   5.08     9.30     3.2           2  
#>  8 Acer rubrum  10.4     15.2     28.3          10.1
#>  9 Acer rubrum   6.10     8.53     5.5           2.5
#> 10 Acer rubrum   6.86    12.3     10.6           4.5
#> # i 78 more rows
\end{verbatim}

Say we want to add facets for these two tree component weights. To do so we need to make the messy \texttt{fef} data tidy (it's messy because the component weights are not held in a single column, i.e., they are in wide format). Referring to Section \ref{tidyrReshape} we'll tidy up \texttt{fef} by moving component weights from wide to long format as follows.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{fef\_long \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{fef }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{pivot\_longer}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{cols =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Stem\_kg"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Otherwoody\_kg"}\NormalTok{),}
               \DataTypeTok{names\_to =} \StringTok{"Component"}\NormalTok{,}
               \DataTypeTok{values\_to =} \StringTok{"Weight\_kg"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{fef\_long }\CommentTok{\# Take a look.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 176 x 5
#>    Species     DBH_cm Height_m Component     Weight_kg
#>    <fct>        <dbl>    <dbl> <chr>             <dbl>
#>  1 Acer rubrum   15.2     14.6 Stem_kg            54.7
#>  2 Acer rubrum   15.2     14.6 Otherwoody_kg      50.4
#>  3 Acer rubrum   17.5     14.6 Stem_kg            62.3
#>  4 Acer rubrum   17.5     14.6 Otherwoody_kg      60.8
#>  5 Acer rubrum   16.3     14.6 Stem_kg            73.3
#>  6 Acer rubrum   16.3     14.6 Otherwoody_kg      41.2
#>  7 Acer rubrum   16.5     14.9 Stem_kg            53.6
#>  8 Acer rubrum   16.5     14.9 Otherwoody_kg      52.3
#>  9 Acer rubrum   18.3     15.5 Stem_kg           106. 
#> 10 Acer rubrum   18.3     15.5 Otherwoody_kg      52.5
#> # i 166 more rows
\end{verbatim}

Now we're ready to add facets for component! Notice in the code below, the \texttt{y} in \texttt{aes()} is now set to \texttt{Weight\_kg} and we specify \texttt{Component} as the additional faceting variable in a call to \texttt{facet\_grid()}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef\_long, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Weight\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{facet\_grid}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{vars}\NormalTok{(Species), }\KeywordTok{vars}\NormalTok{(Component))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-318-1} \end{center}

Looks pretty good, but some final tweaks are needed. Notice the row label for \emph{Liriodendron tulipifera} is cut off, that needs fixing. Also let's add better labels to the axes and columns, and reverse the column order (it feels right to have stem come before otherwoody).

Changing the graphic's column order and labels is most easily accomplished by converting the \texttt{Component} faceting variable to a factor, which is done below using a call to \texttt{factor()} within \texttt{mutate()}. The order in which columns appear in the graphic is determined by the order of the factor levels, and the column labels will be the factor values or, if specified, the factor labels. In the code below, both the \texttt{levels} and \texttt{labels} arguments are specified in the \texttt{factor()} function. Defining the factor with \texttt{labels} effectively replaces \texttt{Stem\_kg} with \texttt{Stem} and \texttt{Otherwoody\_kg} with \texttt{Otherwoody} in the newly formed factor.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{fef\_long \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{fef\_long }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{Component =} \KeywordTok{factor}\NormalTok{(Component, }
                            \DataTypeTok{levels =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Stem\_kg"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Otherwoody\_kg"}\NormalTok{),}
                            \DataTypeTok{labels =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Stem"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Otherwoody"}\NormalTok{)))}

\NormalTok{fef\_long }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{glimpse}\NormalTok{() }\CommentTok{\# Check out the Component factor.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> Rows: 176
#> Columns: 5
#> $ Species   <fct> Acer rubrum, Acer rubrum, Acer rubr~
#> $ DBH_cm    <dbl> 15.240, 15.240, 17.526, 17.526, 16.~
#> $ Height_m  <dbl> 14.6304, 14.6304, 14.6304, 14.6304,~
#> $ Component <fct> Stem, Otherwoody, Stem, Otherwoody,~
#> $ Weight_kg <dbl> 54.7, 50.4, 62.3, 60.8, 73.3, 41.2,~
\end{verbatim}

Getting the row names to wrap, and not be cut off, is best accomplished using the \texttt{facet\_grid()} \texttt{labeller} argument as illustrated below. Here, the \texttt{labeller} argument takes an object created using the \texttt{labeller()} function which specifies how the species names should be wrapped onto multiple lines after a given width is exceeded. Column labels can also be specified using the \texttt{labeller} argument, but it's easier to specify the labels when forming the \texttt{Component} factor.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef\_long, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Weight\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{facet\_grid}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{vars}\NormalTok{(Species), }\KeywordTok{vars}\NormalTok{(Component),}
             \DataTypeTok{labeller =} \KeywordTok{labeller}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{Species =} 
                                   \KeywordTok{label\_wrap\_gen}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{width =} \DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }
                                                  \DataTypeTok{multi\_line =} \OtherTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-320-1} \end{center}

As with other topics covered in this chapter, we provide an incomplete tour of facets. These are extremely useful tools for exploring your data and communicating information. We'll explore additional facet functionality in subsequent sections.

\hypertarget{building-graphics-incrementally}{%
\subsection{Building graphics incrementally}\label{building-graphics-incrementally}}

As you've seen, constructing a graphic is an incremental process that often involves trial and error. It can be useful to build a base graphic, to which you add layers and modifying functions. The base graphic and subsequent incremental additions can be saved as a \texttt{ggplot} object. Below we define a base graphic and assign it to \texttt{p}, then update \texttt{p} incrementally once we're happy with the additions. Notice the graphic is not plotted until you run \texttt{p} on its own (which is equivalent to running \texttt{print(p)}).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{p \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{shape =} \DecValTok{21}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{fill =}\NormalTok{ Height\_m))}

\NormalTok{p }\CommentTok{\# Implicit for print(p).}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-321-1} \end{center}

Now you can redefine or modify the point geometry layer, and add more layers and modifying functions to \texttt{p}. Below, we try out a color gradient to see if we like it.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Don\textquotesingle{}t like the colors.}
\NormalTok{p }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{scale\_fill\_gradient}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{low =} \StringTok{"blue"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{high =} \StringTok{"green"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-322-1} \end{center}

Try some different colors.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Better colors, save it and move on.}
\NormalTok{p \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{scale\_fill\_gradient}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{low =} \StringTok{"white"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{high =} \StringTok{"seagreen"}\NormalTok{)  }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Now adjust scales and themes to your liking.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{p \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"Height (m)"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{x =} \StringTok{"DBH (cm)"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Stem (kg)"}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{theme\_bw}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{text =} \KeywordTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{))}

\NormalTok{p }\CommentTok{\# Looks good!}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-324-1} \end{center}

\hypertarget{ggplotPatchwork}{%
\subsection{Arranging graphics}\label{ggplotPatchwork}}

We often want to show two or more graphics side by side to compare and communicate information. Using facets is one approach, but limited to comparing the same data representation across one or two faceting variables. There are several packages that provide functions for arranging multiple \texttt{ggplot()} graphics, including \texttt{cowplot} \citep{cowplot} and \texttt{patchwork} \citep{patchwork}. Here, we briefly demonstrate the \texttt{patchwork} package, because it's versatile, simple, and adopts \texttt{ggplot2}'s \texttt{+} operator to add plots together. In the words of the package author Thomas Lin Pedersen, ``the goal of patchwork is to make it ridiculously simple to combine separate ggplots into the same graphic.''

Below we make several \texttt{ggplot} objects then modify and arrange them in different ways to demonstrate \texttt{patchwork}'s most basic capabilities. Install the \texttt{patchwork} package via \texttt{install.packages("patchwork")}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }
              \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_histogram}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{ggtitle}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Plot 1"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{ \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }
              \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{ggtitle}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Plot 2"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{ \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef\_long, }
              \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Weight\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{facet\_wrap}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{vars}\NormalTok{(Component)) }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{ggtitle}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Plot 3"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{ \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ fef, }
              \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Stem\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Species)) }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_smooth}\NormalTok{() }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{ggtitle}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Plot 4"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Add two graphics together using the \texttt{+} operator.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{library}\NormalTok{(patchwork)}

\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-326-1} \end{center}

When adding graphics together, the last added \texttt{ggplot} is active and will receive any added layers or modifying functions.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}This will appear in the right plot\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-327-1} \end{center}

Parentheses can be used to control order of operations. For example, to add layers or modifying functions to a \texttt{ggplot} that is not the last \texttt{ggplot}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(p\_}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}This will appear in the left plot\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-328-1} \end{center}

By default, \texttt{patchwork} tries to keep the layout square and fill it out by row order.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{3} \OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{4}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-329-1} \end{center}

Control the layout with the addition of the \texttt{plot\_layout()} function.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{3} \OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{4} \OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{plot\_layout}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{nrow =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{byrow =} \OtherTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-330-1} \end{center}

In addition to controlling placement via \texttt{plot\_layout()}, you can use the \texttt{\textbar{}} operator to place plots beside each other and the \texttt{/} operator to stack them.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{/}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-331-1} \end{center}

Using parentheses with the placement operators allows you to create nested layouts.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{|}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{(p\_}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{/}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{p\_}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-332-1} \end{center}

This is a very brief overview of some of \texttt{patchwork}'s capabilities. See its manual pages, vignette via \texttt{vignette("patchwork")}, and on-line reference at \url{https://patchwork.data-imaginist.com} for much more, including how to add annotation, collect legends into one place, remove duplicate legends, create overlays and insets, and further control layout and alignment.

\hypertarget{saving-graphics}{%
\subsection{Saving graphics}\label{saving-graphics}}

We often want to export our graphics for use in an external document or share with colleagues. There are several ways to save graphics in a variety of file formats. One approach is to use \texttt{ggsave()}, see \texttt{?ggplot2::ggsave()}. The \texttt{plot} argument default in \texttt{ggsave()} is to save the most recent graphic; however, you can also give \texttt{plot} a \texttt{ggplot} object, like the ones created in the previous section. The \texttt{device} argument allows you to choose among a variety of vector (e.g., ``eps'', ``ps'', ``pdv'', ``svg'') or raster (e.g., ``jpeg'', ``tiff'', ``png'', ``bmp'', ``wmf'') formats.\footnote{Vector files use point, line, curve, and polygon elements, whereas raster files depict these elements using shaded pixels. Vector images are often preferred for publication quality graphics because they can be edited, scaled without loss of definition, and provide crisper detail.}

\hypertarget{exploring-different-graphics}{%
\section{Exploring different graphics}\label{exploring-different-graphics}}

We explore \texttt{ggplot2} further using the FACE dataset introduced in Section \ref{face}. Recall, the Aspen FACE was designed to assess the effect of increasing tropospheric ozone (O\(_3\)) and carbon dioxide (CO\(_2\)) concentration on structure and functioning of northern forest ecosystems.

As noted previously, \texttt{ggplot2} functions are designed to work with tidy data; therefore, we'll use the tidy FACE tibble \texttt{face\_long} created at the end of Section \ref{tidyrSeparate}. You can either recreate this tibble following the code in Section \ref{tidyrReshape} or read it into \texttt{face\_long} from the ``datasets/FACE\_long.csv'' file using the code below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{face\_long \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"datasets/FACE\_long.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{face\_long }\CommentTok{\# Take a look.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 9,955 x 6
#>      Rep Treat Clone    ID  Year Height_cm
#>    <dbl> <dbl> <dbl> <dbl> <dbl>     <dbl>
#>  1     1     1     8    45  2001        NA
#>  2     1     1     8    45  2002        NA
#>  3     1     1     8    45  2003        NA
#>  4     1     1     8    45  2004        NA
#>  5     1     1     8    45  2005        NA
#>  6     1     1   216    44  2001       547
#>  7     1     1   216    44  2002       622
#>  8     1     1   216    44  2003       715
#>  9     1     1   216    44  2004       716
#> 10     1     1   216    44  2005       817
#> # i 9,945 more rows
\end{verbatim}

Here, the \texttt{Rep}, \texttt{Treat}, and \texttt{Clone} columns are part of the experimental design, \texttt{ID} is a unique tree identification number, \texttt{Year} is the year in which the tree's height was measured, and the recorded height is held in \texttt{Height\_cm}. As mentioned previously, the FACE data are longitudinal, meaning observational units, trees in this case, are measured over time. Most longitudinal data are collected to understand how observational units respond to different treatments. The FACE was designed to look at how tree growth responds to elevated tropospheric O\(_3\) and CO\(_2\). We'll explore this question using a series of graphics.

We begin by adding a bit more information to \texttt{face\_long}. As the name suggests, the \texttt{Treat} variable records each tree's treatment. Trees were assigned to one of four treatments for the study's duration. The treatments are coded 1 (control), 2 (elevated CO\(_2\)), 3 (elevated O\(_3\)), 4 (elevated CO\(_2\) and O\(_3\)). Let's add this more descriptive information to \texttt{Treat} by converting the column to a factor and associating treatment numeric code levels with treatment labels. We also convert \texttt{Year} to a factor.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{face\_long \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{face\_long }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{Treat =} \KeywordTok{factor}\NormalTok{(Treat, }\DataTypeTok{levels =} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{:}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}
                        \DataTypeTok{labels =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Control"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Elevated CO2"}\NormalTok{, }
                                   \StringTok{"Elevated O3"}\NormalTok{, }
                                   \StringTok{"Elevated CO2 and O3"}\NormalTok{)),}
         \DataTypeTok{Year =} \KeywordTok{factor}\NormalTok{(Year))}

\NormalTok{face\_long }\CommentTok{\# Take a look at the new factors.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 9,955 x 6
#>      Rep Treat   Clone    ID Year  Height_cm
#>    <dbl> <fct>   <dbl> <dbl> <fct>     <dbl>
#>  1     1 Control     8    45 2001         NA
#>  2     1 Control     8    45 2002         NA
#>  3     1 Control     8    45 2003         NA
#>  4     1 Control     8    45 2004         NA
#>  5     1 Control     8    45 2005         NA
#>  6     1 Control   216    44 2001        547
#>  7     1 Control   216    44 2002        622
#>  8     1 Control   216    44 2003        715
#>  9     1 Control   216    44 2004        716
#> 10     1 Control   216    44 2005        817
#> # i 9,945 more rows
\end{verbatim}

Let's get a little better sense of these data. We can see from the output above that \texttt{face\_long} has 9955 rows and there are some \texttt{NA} \texttt{Height\_cm} values. Let's figure out the number of trees, number of missing height measurements, and number of trees in each each treatment (recall, trees are assigned to only one treatment).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{face\_long }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{summarize}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{n\_tree =} \KeywordTok{n\_distinct}\NormalTok{(ID), }\CommentTok{\# Trees in the study.}
            \DataTypeTok{n\_na\_heights =} \KeywordTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(Height\_cm))) }\CommentTok{\# Number of NA.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 1 x 2
#>   n_tree n_na_heights
#>    <int>        <int>
#> 1   1991         1344
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{face\_long }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(Treat) }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{n\_trees =} \KeywordTok{n\_distinct}\NormalTok{(ID)) }\CommentTok{\# Trees in each treatment.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 4 x 2
#>   Treat               n_trees
#>   <fct>                 <int>
#> 1 Control                 498
#> 2 Elevated CO2            497
#> 3 Elevated O3             497
#> 4 Elevated CO2 and O3     499
\end{verbatim}

Some working hypotheses that motivated the FACE study were that elevated atmospheric CO\(_2\) could increase tree growth rate, while increased O\(_3\) could damage leaf tissue and decrease growth rate. Let's see if the data shows a treatment effect on height growth over time.

Begin by plotting each tree's growth over years as a line and color the line by treatment. Given what we learned about aesthetics and geometries in the previous section the code below might be a logical starting point.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ face\_long, }
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Year, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Height\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Treat)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-337-1} \end{center}

While the graphic is not what we're aiming for, it's close. It's actually drawing a line connecting all height measurements within year and treatment. As it stands, there is nothing telling \texttt{geom\_line()} that sets of height measurements are associated with individual trees over years. This information is provided using the \texttt{group} aesthetic, see \texttt{?ggplot2::aes\_group\_order}, via \texttt{group\ =\ ID} in \texttt{aes()} below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ face\_long, }
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Year, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Height\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Treat,}
                     \DataTypeTok{group =}\NormalTok{ ID)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> Warning: Removed 1336 rows containing missing values
#> (`geom_line()`).
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-338-1} \end{center}

Okay, this is looking better.\footnote{Notice the warning message that results from this code. The warning for this graphic says there are 1336 rows removed due to missing, i.e., \texttt{NA}, values. We know from exploring these data there are 1344 \texttt{NA} \texttt{Height\_cm} and no missing years. So, we might wonder why the warning doesn't say 1344 rows removed. The answer is in the \texttt{geom\_line()} manual page under the missing value handling section, which notes that \texttt{NA}s at the beginning or end of lines are removed without a warning. This accounts for the disparity of 8 \texttt{NA}s removed.} Each line traces a tree's growth over time and color shows the tree's treatment. Perhaps if there were fewer trees we'd be able to notice some treatment effect. So perhaps the better approach is to summarize tree growth trends within each treatment. The \texttt{stat\_summary()} function is made for just such a task---it summarizes \texttt{y} at discrete values of \texttt{x} using the function specified by the \texttt{fun} argument and displays the result using the geometry specified by the \texttt{geom} argument.

The call to \texttt{stat\_summary()} below inherits the aesthetics defined in \texttt{aes()}, computes the mean height measurement for year and treatment combinations, and displays the result as a line geometry.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ face\_long, }
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Year, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Height\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Treat, }
                     \DataTypeTok{group =}\NormalTok{ Treat)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{stat\_summary}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{geom =} \StringTok{"line"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{fun =}\NormalTok{ mean)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> Warning: Removed 1344 rows containing non-finite
#> values (`stat_summary()`).
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-339-1} \end{center}

Each line in the graphic traces mean growth within each treatment over years.\footnote{Notice the \texttt{ggplot()} warning says it removed 1344 rows containing non-finite values. These rows correspond to the 1344 \texttt{NA} \texttt{Height\_cm} values.}

It's instructive to take this one step farther. Let's draw the individual trees below the mean growth lines. We'll make the individual tree lines semi-transparent so they don't dominate the scene. To do so, we keep \texttt{x}, \texttt{y}, and \texttt{color} aesthetics in \texttt{ggplot()}, map the \texttt{geom\_line()} to \texttt{group\ =\ ID}, and map \texttt{stat\_summary()} to \texttt{group\ =\ Treat}. Setting transparency is done via the \texttt{alpha} aesthetic.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ face\_long, }
       \DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Year, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Height\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ Treat)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{group =}\NormalTok{ ID), }\DataTypeTok{alpha =} \FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{stat\_summary}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{geom =} \StringTok{"line"}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{fun =}\NormalTok{ mean, }\DataTypeTok{mapping =} \KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{group =}\NormalTok{ Treat), }
               \DataTypeTok{size =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> Warning: Removed 1344 rows containing non-finite
#> values (`stat_summary()`).
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
#> Warning: Removed 1336 rows containing missing values
#> (`geom_line()`).
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-340-1} \end{center}

The mean lines suggest there are some differences among the treatments. Specifically, elevated CO\(_2\) seems to increase overall height compared with the other three treatments. Elevated O\(_3\) seems to decrease overall height and growth rate appears to decrease beyond 2003, relative to the other treatments. The control and elevated CO\(_2\) and O\(_3\) height and growth trends are about the same. However, as the individual tree growth lines suggest, there is large variability in individual tree response to the various treatments. This variability needs to be taken into account to draw statistically valid conclusions about treatment effect. Formal analysis of the FACE data and results from similar studies have shown O\(_3\)'s negative growth effects on tree seedlings. However, long-term positive growth effects of elevated CO\(_2\) have not been clearly demonstrated, especially while in the presence of elevated O\(_3\) and other environmental factors that negatively impact growth, see, e.g., \citet{McLaughlin2007}, \citet{Karnosky2007}, \citet{Davis2022}.

\hypertarget{ggplotBarCharts}{%
\section{Visualizing distributions}\label{ggplotBarCharts}}

There are a number of geometries used to display data distributions. The appropriate \texttt{ggplot2} geometry for the job will depend on whether the distribution is defined by one or more variables, data types of the variables, and how variables are to summarized.

In this section we focus on a limited set of geometries most often used to summarize forestry data distributions. In particular, we use geometries that create bar charts to visualize forest inventory data by summarizing a continuous variable (e.g., volume, basal area) or discrete variable (e.g., occurrence) by one or more categorical variables, e.g., DBH class, species. We begin by illustrating the geometries using a very simple toy dataset, then provide an example using the Elk County timber cruise dataset introduced in Section \ref{elk}.

Below is a toy dataset comprising six trees and their associated species code (\texttt{Spp}), DBH (\texttt{DBH\_cm}), and biomass (\texttt{Bio\_kg}).\footnote{Species codes are from the PLANTS database \citep{PLANTSDB} with FAGR (\emph{Fagus grandifolia}), ACRU (\emph{Acer saccharum}), and BEPA (\emph{Betula papyrifera}) and biomass was estimated from DBH using equations provided in \citet{jenkins2004}).} We also add a 2 cm DBH class variable with intervals \([18, 20)\), \([20, 22)\), \([22, 24)\), and \([24, 26]\) using \texttt{mutate()} and the handy \texttt{cut\_width()} function, introduced in Section \ref{standStockTabs}, and place it to the right of the \texttt{DBH\_cm} column.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{tbl \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{Spp =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"ACRU"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"ACRU"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"FAGR"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"FAGR"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"BEPA"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"BEPA"}\NormalTok{),}
              \DataTypeTok{DBH\_cm =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{24}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{22}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{24}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{18}\NormalTok{),}
              \DataTypeTok{Bio\_kg =} \KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{272}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{221}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{173}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{306}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{176}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{138}\NormalTok{))}

\CommentTok{\# Add DBH class.}
\NormalTok{tbl \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{tbl }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(tbl, }
         \DataTypeTok{DBH\_2cm\_class =} \KeywordTok{cut\_width}\NormalTok{(DBH\_cm, }\DataTypeTok{width =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }
                                   \DataTypeTok{center =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{closed =} \StringTok{"left"}\NormalTok{), }
         \DataTypeTok{.after =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_cm)}

\NormalTok{tbl }\CommentTok{\# Take a look.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 6 x 4
#>   Spp   DBH_cm DBH_2cm_class Bio_kg
#>   <chr>  <dbl> <fct>          <dbl>
#> 1 ACRU      24 [22,24]          272
#> 2 ACRU      22 [22,24]          221
#> 3 FAGR      19 [18,20)          173
#> 4 FAGR      24 [22,24]          306
#> 5 BEPA      20 [20,22)          176
#> 6 BEPA      18 [18,20)          138
\end{verbatim}

There are two types of bar chart geometries that differ by how bar height is computed.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \texttt{geom\_bar()} makes bar height proportional to the number of discrete value occurrences, i.e., counts, in the given variable (or if the \texttt{weight} aesthetic is supplied, the sum of the weight values). These counts (or sums) can be partitioned by other discrete variables. The \texttt{geom\_bar()} function uses the \texttt{stat\_count()} stat function which counts discrete value occurrences.
\item
  \texttt{geom\_col()} makes bar height proportional to the value in the given variable. These sums can be partitioned by one or more discrete variables. The \texttt{geom\_col()} function uses the \texttt{stat\_identity()} stat function which leaves values unchanged.
\end{enumerate}

The code below uses \texttt{geom\_bar()} to show tree count by \texttt{DBH\_class} and tree count by species and \texttt{DBH\_class}. We place the resulting bar charts side by side to facilitate comparison.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(tbl, }\KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_2cm\_class)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{()}

\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(tbl, }\KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_2cm\_class, }\DataTypeTok{fill =}\NormalTok{ Spp)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-343-1} \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-343-2} \end{center}

Notice in the right bar chart above, the default behavior is to stack species counts, i.e., for the \([18,20)\) cm DBH class there is one FAGR and one BEPA, and in the \([22,24]\) cm DBH class there are two ACRU and one FAGR. This default behavior is controlled using \emph{position adjustment} of elements within a layer. Three adjustments apply primarily to bars:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{position\_stack()} stack overlapping bars on top of each other (default),
\item
  \texttt{position\_fill()} stack overlapping bars, scaling so the top is always at 1,
\item
  \texttt{position\_dodge()} place overlapping bars side by side.
\end{itemize}

Each position adjustment is illustrated below below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{bars \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(tbl, }\KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_2cm\_class, }\DataTypeTok{fill =}\NormalTok{ Spp)) }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{xlab}\NormalTok{(}\OtherTok{NULL}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{ylab}\NormalTok{(}\OtherTok{NULL}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"none"}\NormalTok{) }

\NormalTok{bars }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{() }\CommentTok{\# Default, same as geom\_bar(position = "stack")}

\NormalTok{bars }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{position =} \StringTok{"fill"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{bars }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{position =} \StringTok{"dodge"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.3\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-344-1} \includegraphics[width=0.3\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-344-2} \includegraphics[width=0.3\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-344-3} \end{center}

All three position adjustments above have use in inventory summaries. Select the one that best conveys the information you wish to communicate. For example, the middle bar chart (i.e., \texttt{position\ =\ "fill"}) shows species \emph{relative abundance} by DBH class. Relative abundance is a component of diversity and is a measure of how common or rare a species is relative to other species. This chart shows FAGR and BEPA are equally abundant in the \([18,20)\) cm class, BEPA dominates the \([20,22)\) cm class, and ACRU is more abundant than FAGR in the \([22,24]\) cm classes.

Next, let's use \texttt{geom\_col()} to make two bar charts that show total biomass by \texttt{DBH\_class} and biomass by species and \texttt{DBH\_class}. Notice, \texttt{geom\_col()} requires a \texttt{y} aesthetic which is summed by \texttt{x} aesthetic class and any other discrete aesthetic provided to \texttt{color} or \texttt{fill}. We again take the default \texttt{position\ =\ "stack"} for each bar chart and place the charts side by side to facilitate comparison.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(tbl, }\KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_2cm\_class, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Bio\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_col}\NormalTok{()}

\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(tbl, }\KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_2cm\_class, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Bio\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{fill =}\NormalTok{ Spp)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_col}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-345-1} \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-345-2} \end{center}

The figures above look similar to those from \texttt{geom\_bar()}, but now the bar height is the sum of \texttt{Bio\_kg}.\footnote{As an aside, you might notice some horizontal white lines in the bars. They are artifacts from how the data are internally summarized and from omitting the \texttt{color} aesthetic (which determines bar segment's outline color). So the line's you're seeing are the default \texttt{color\ =\ NA} aesthetic. To remove those pesky lines, so as not to confuse your reader, match the \texttt{color} and \texttt{fill} argument values, e.g., in the first chart above add \texttt{color\ =\ "grey35"} to \texttt{geom\_col()} and for the second chart add \texttt{color\ =\ Spp} to \texttt{aes()}. Hopefully this default will change in future \texttt{ggplot2} versions.}

Setting the positional adjustment to \texttt{fill} for the bar chart shows a measure of species \emph{relative dominance} by DBH class. Like relative abundance, relative dominance is a component of diversity and is a measure of how dominant a species is relative to other species, based on some continuous variable like biomass, volume, or basal area. The bar chart below shows relative dominance measured using biomass by species.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(tbl, }\KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_2cm\_class, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Bio\_kg, }\DataTypeTok{fill =}\NormalTok{ Spp)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_col}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{position =} \StringTok{"fill"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-346-1} \end{center}

Above we see that for the \([18,20)\) cm class FAGR is slightly dominant over BEPA (i.e., has slightly more biomass per acre), BEPA is the only and hence dominant species in the \([20,22)\) cm class, and ACRU dominates FAGR in the \([20,23]\) cm class.

Now, let's look at relative abundance and relative dominance without breaking down by DBH class.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(tbl, }\KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Spp)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_bar}\NormalTok{()}

\KeywordTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(tbl, }\KeywordTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Spp, }\DataTypeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Bio\_kg)) }\OperatorTok{+}
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{geom\_col}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-347-1} \includegraphics[width=0.45\linewidth]{FD20_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-347-2} \end{center}

The left bar chart shows that species are equally abundant, because there are two of each. The right plot shows ACRU has slightly more biomass than FAGR and is hence the dominate species, based on this measure of dominance.

\hypertarget{elk-county-timber-cruise-bar-charts}{%
\subsection{Elk County timber cruise bar charts}\label{elk-county-timber-cruise-bar-charts}}

Let's return to the Elk County timber cruise dataset introduced in Section \ref{elk} and developed in more detail at the beginning of the Section \ref{exercisesTidyr}. Much of what is covered here builds on stand and stock tables covered in Section \ref{standStockTabs}, in fact several graphics we create are simply visual representations of tabular summaries created previously.

Our initial focus is on 1-dimensional distributions of continuous variables summarized by one or more categorical variable. Begin by reading the per acre estimates for acceptable growing stock into \texttt{elk\_ags} and adding the DBH class factor (\texttt{DBH\_2in\_range}) which more explicitly defines the two inch DBH class used when the data were recorded in the field.\footnote{You could use \texttt{DBH\_2in} in the subsequent bar charts, but defining the DBH classes as factors that use interval notation eases interpretation.} Refer to the beginning of Section \ref{exercisesTidyr} for a description of these data and column definitions. Here, per acre number of trees (\texttt{Trees\_ac}), basal area (\texttt{BA\_ac}), and number of 16 foot logs (\texttt{Logs\_ac}) are estimated for each observed species and two inch DBH class combination.\footnote{Methods to generate these estimates from tree measurement data will be covered in Chapter \ref{forestStat}.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{elk\_ags \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"datasets/elk\_spp\_dbh\_estimates.csv"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{elk\_ags \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{elk\_ags }\OperatorTok{\%\textgreater{}\%}\StringTok{ }
\StringTok{  }\KeywordTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{DBH\_2in\_range =} \KeywordTok{cut\_width}\NormalTok{(DBH\_2in, }\DataTypeTok{width =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }
                                   \DataTypeTok{boundary =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{closed =} \StringTok{"left"}\NormalTok{),}
         \DataTypeTok{.after =}\NormalTok{ DBH\_2in)}

\NormalTok{elk\_ags}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> # A tibble: 43 x 6
#>    Species DBH_2in DBH_2in_range Trees_ac BA_ac Logs_ac
#>    <chr>     <dbl> <fct>            <dbl> <dbl>   <dbl>
#>  1 Beech        12 [11,13)         1.33   1.04    1.33 
#>  2 Beech        14 [13,15)         0.585  0.625   0.682
#>  3 Beech        16 [15,17)         0.448  0.625   0.746
#>  4 Beech        20 [19,21)         0.0955 0.208   0.143
#>  5 Black ~      12 [11,13)         1.06   0.833   1.06 
#>  6 Black ~      14 [13,15)         0.974  1.04    1.07 
#>  7 Black ~      16 [15,17)         0.895  1.25    1.42 
#>  8 Black ~      18 [17,19)         0.118  0.208   0.236
#>  9 Black ~      20 [19,21)         0.0955 0.208   0.191
#> 10 Black ~      12 [11,13)         0.796  0.625   0.796
#> # i 33 more rows
\end{verbatim}
