\NormalTok{  i \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\NormalTok{i }\OperatorTok{+}\StringTok{ }\DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> [1] 1.5
#> [1] 4.5
#> [1] 3.6
#> [1] 4.4
#> [1] 5.3
#> [1] 5.1
#> [1] 11.1
#> [1] 0.9
#> [1] 2.3
#> [1] 3.5
\end{verbatim}

Let's break down each component of the \texttt{while} loop:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  We first set \texttt{i\ \textless{}-\ 1}. This will serve as our indexing variable that will keep track of which element we are on.
\item
  Our condition inside the \texttt{while} loop is \texttt{i\ \textless{}=\ 10}, which means we will perform the expression inside the loop until \texttt{i} is equal to or greater than 10.
\item
  Inside the loop is our expression \texttt{print(pef\_p11\$DBH\_in{[}i{]})}, telling R to print the ith DBH value.
\item
  Also included inside the loop is \texttt{i\ \textless{}-\ i\ +\ 1}, which serves to increase \texttt{i} each time we print a value in the loop.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{forLoops}{%
\subsection{A for loop}\label{forLoops}}

A \texttt{for} loop has the form:

\begin{verbatim}
for (variable in vector) {
  expression
}
\end{verbatim}

The \texttt{for} loop sets the \texttt{variable} equal to each element of the \texttt{vector} in succession, and evaluates the \texttt{expression} each time. As an alternative to the \texttt{head} function and the \texttt{while} loop we used above, we can print the first 10 DBH values in \texttt{pef\_p11} using a \texttt{for} loop:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{:}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{) \{}
  \KeywordTok{print}\NormalTok{(pef\_p11}\OperatorTok{$}\NormalTok{DBH\_in[i])}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> [1] 1.5
#> [1] 4.5
#> [1] 3.6
#> [1] 4.4
#> [1] 5.3
#> [1] 5.1
#> [1] 11.1
#> [1] 0.9
#> [1] 2.3
#> [1] 3.5
\end{verbatim}

We see that our variable \texttt{i} is set to start at 1 and iterate by one through the values to 10 (\texttt{1:10}). Inside the loop is the same expression we saw in the \texttt{while} loop to print the ith DBH value.

Now let's return to our example of computing the AGB for each tree in the \texttt{pef\_p11} data set. We could do this one by one for each tree in the dataset:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{agb}\NormalTok{(pef\_p11}\OperatorTok{$}\NormalTok{DBH\_in[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], pef\_p11}\OperatorTok{$}\NormalTok{common\_name[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> [1] 2.1832
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{agb}\NormalTok{(pef\_p11}\OperatorTok{$}\NormalTok{DBH\_in[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{], pef\_p11}\OperatorTok{$}\NormalTok{common\_name[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{])}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> [1] 33.344
\end{verbatim}

Doing this for the 253 trees in \texttt{pef\_p11} would be highly inefficient. We can make this a bit easier by using a \texttt{for} loop.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{pef\_p11}\OperatorTok{$}\NormalTok{AGB \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\OtherTok{NA}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{:}\KeywordTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(pef\_p11)) \{}
\NormalTok{  pef\_p11}\OperatorTok{$}\NormalTok{AGB[i] \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{agb}\NormalTok{(pef\_p11}\OperatorTok{$}\NormalTok{DBH\_in[i], pef\_p11}\OperatorTok{$}\NormalTok{common\_name[i])}
\NormalTok{\}}
\KeywordTok{head}\NormalTok{(pef\_p11}\OperatorTok{$}\NormalTok{AGB, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#>  [1]   2.1832  33.3444  19.1668  31.5359  50.0449
#>  [6]  45.4889 313.3327       NA   6.3057  17.8728
\end{verbatim}

While this was much more efficient than individually running the function for each tree in the data set, using a \texttt{for} loop to accomplish this task in R is not the most efficient way. Instead, we can use \texttt{apply()}.

\hypertarget{apply}{%
\section{\texorpdfstring{The \texttt{apply()} function}{The apply() function}}\label{apply}}

In the previous section we used a \texttt{for} loop to create a new column (\texttt{AGB}) to our \texttt{pef\_p11} data frame. \texttt{for} loops are sometimes difficult to understand and lead to potentially more errors that go unnoticed. In many situations, we can replace \texttt{for} loops and produce more reproducible code by using \texttt{apply()}, which, along with a series of other functions based on \texttt{apply()}, is one of the most commonly-used functions in R. This function applies a user-defined function to a data frame's rows, columns, or both rows and columns.\footnote{Note \texttt{apply()} can also be used with higher-dimensional arrays, in which functions can be applied to any subset of the dimensions at once.} The arguments are:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{X} the data frame of interest.
\item
  \texttt{MARGIN} the rows (\texttt{MARGIN\ =\ 1}), columns (\texttt{MARGIN\ =\ 2}), or rows and columns (\texttt{MARGIN\ =\ c(1,2)}) the function in the third argument \texttt{FUN} should be applied to.
\item
  \texttt{FUN} the function to be applied.
\end{enumerate}

For example, consider the FEF data set (Section \ref{fef}), which is imported into R in the following code chunk

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{fef\_trees \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\KeywordTok{read.table}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{file =} \StringTok{"datasets/FEF\_trees.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
            \DataTypeTok{header =} \OtherTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{sep =} \StringTok{","}\NormalTok{)}
\KeywordTok{str}\NormalTok{(fef\_trees)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> 'data.frame':    88 obs. of  18 variables:
#>  $ watershed        : int  3 3 3 3 3 3 3 3 3 3 ...
#>  $ year             : int  1991 1991 1991 1991 1991 1992 1992 1992 1992 1992 ...
#>  $ plot             : int  29 33 35 39 44 26 26 26 48 48 ...
#>  $ species          : chr  "Acer rubrum" "Acer rubrum" "Acer rubrum" "Acer rubrum" ...
#>  $ dbh_in           : num  6 6.9 6.4 6.5 7.2 3.1 2 4.1 2.4 2.7 ...
#>  $ height_ft        : num  48 48 48 49 51 40 30.5 50 28 40.4 ...
#>  $ stem_green_kg    : num  92.2 102.3 124.4 91.7 186.2 ...
#>  $ top_green_kg     : num  13.1 23.1 8.7 39 8.9 0.9 0.9 8.6 0.7 5 ...
#>  $ smbranch_green_kg: num  30.5 23.5 22.3 22.5 25.4 1.9 2.2 8 3.7 3.8 ...
#>  $ lgbranch_green_kg: num  48.4 57.7 44.1 35.5 65.1 1.5 0.6 4 0.5 1.8 ...
#>  $ allwoody_green_kg: num  184 207 200 189 286 ...
#>  $ leaves_green_kg  : num  16.1 12.9 16.5 12 22.4 0.9 1 6.1 2.5 1.6 ...
#>  $ stem_dry_kg      : num  54.7 62.3 73.3 53.6 106.4 ...
#>  $ top_dry_kg       : num  7.1 12.4 4.6 21.3 4.7 0.5 0.5 4.4 0.4 2.7 ...
#>  $ smbranch_dry_kg  : num  15.3 14.8 11.5 11.2 11.7 1.1 1.2 3.6 1.8 0.8 ...
#>  $ lgbranch_dry_kg  : num  28 33.6 25.1 19.8 36.1 0.9 0.3 2.1 0.3 1 ...
#>  $ allwoody_dry_kg  : num  105 123 114 106 159 ...
#>  $ leaves_dry_kg    : num  6.1 4.6 6.1 4.2 7.9 0.3 0.3 1.9 0.8 0.5 ...
\end{verbatim}

This data sets contains numerous measurements on tree characteristics useful for developing allometric equations. Suppose we want to compute the standard deviation of each measured variable (stored in columns 5 through 18). We can do this with one line of code using \texttt{apply()}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{apply}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{X =}\NormalTok{ fef\_trees[, }\DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{:}\DecValTok{18}\NormalTok{], }\DataTypeTok{MARGIN =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{FUN =}\NormalTok{ sd)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#>            dbh_in         height_ft     stem_green_kg 
#>            2.0580           10.6533           66.2194 
#>      top_green_kg smbranch_green_kg lgbranch_green_kg 
#>           10.5948           11.8596           27.5178 
#> allwoody_green_kg   leaves_green_kg       stem_dry_kg 
#>          109.0185            7.7560           36.5512 
#>        top_dry_kg   smbranch_dry_kg   lgbranch_dry_kg 
#>            5.6739            6.1836           15.4168 
#>   allwoody_dry_kg     leaves_dry_kg 
#>           60.1813            2.8747
\end{verbatim}

Following our discussion in Section (\ref{callingFunctions}, as long as argument values are given in the correct order, the corresponding argument names don't need to be included. After consulting the \texttt{apply()} manual page you'll see \texttt{X}, \texttt{MARGIN} and \texttt{FUN} are the first three arguments, so the code \texttt{apply(fef\_trees{[},\ 5:18{]},\ 2,\ sd)} is equivalent to the code above. While this could also be accomplished using a \texttt{for} loop, the \texttt{apply()} function provides a more concise and faster way of accomplishing the same task.

\hypertarget{using-user-defined-functions-with-apply}{%
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Using user-defined functions with \texttt{apply()}}{Using user-defined functions with apply()}}\label{using-user-defined-functions-with-apply}}

We just learned how to use \texttt{apply()} with built-in R functions like \texttt{sd} and \texttt{mean}. We can greatly expand the power of the \texttt{apply()} function by using our own user-defined functions within \texttt{apply()}. Now that we know how to write our own R functions, this becomes a trivial task.

Suppose we want to calculate the total number of non-missing values for all columns in the \texttt{pef\_p11} data frame. Let's first write a function that will calculate the total number of non-missing values in a vector of values:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sum\_no\_na \textless{}{-}}\StringTok{ }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(values) \{}
   \KeywordTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\OperatorTok{!}\KeywordTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(values))}
\NormalTok{\}}
\KeywordTok{sum\_no\_na}\NormalTok{(}\KeywordTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\OtherTok{NA}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#> [1] 4
\end{verbatim}

Notice that we do not explicitly use \texttt{return()} in our function, but the function returns the values from \texttt{sum(!is.na(values))}. In R, if a function does not have an explicit \texttt{return} statement, the function will return the last value in the body of the function, which in our case is \texttt{sum(!is.na(values))}. Now we can directly supply our newly created function to \texttt{apply()} for use with the \texttt{pef\_p11} data frame:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{apply}\NormalTok{(pef\_p11, }\DataTypeTok{MARGIN =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, sum\_no\_na)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
#>             year PEF_species_code           DBH_in 
#>              253              253              253 
#>      common_name  scientific_name    USFS_FIA_code 
#>              253              250              253 
#>              AGB 
#>              211
\end{verbatim}

Combining user-defined functions with the \texttt{apply()} function is an efficient R coding technique which we use nearly every day.

\hypertarget{relatives-of-apply}{%
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Relatives of \texttt{apply()}}{Relatives of apply()}}\label{relatives-of-apply}}

\texttt{apply()} is the basic function for an entire family of related functions that similarly perform repeated observations to R objects. Such extensions of \texttt{apply()} include \texttt{lapply()}, \texttt{sapply()}, and \texttt{mapply()}. Each of these functions are used to perform a series of tasks simultaneously across different types of R objects. Functions like \texttt{lapply()} and \texttt{sapply()} are particularly useful when working with lists (Section \ref{lists}). We encourage you to explore the help pages for these functions if you find yourself often writing \texttt{for} loops to accomplish seemingly simple, but repetitive, tasks. Further, we will soon see that many tasks performed by \texttt{for} loops or the \texttt{apply()} family of functions can also be accomplished using intuitive functions from the \texttt{dplyr} package after transforming data sets into tidy format (Chapter \ref{tidyverse}).

\hypertarget{summaryFunctions}{%
\section{Summary}\label{summaryFunctions}}

In this chapter we introduced many essential programming techniques that will take your R coding and data analysis to the next steps. Much of our discussion focused around how to write your own functions. We applied this knowledge to make a function that computed species-specific above ground biomass. We then briefly discussed the two most common types of loops, \texttt{while} loops and \texttt{for} loops, and how to use them in R. We emphasized that loops are less important in R as a result of vectorized functions like \texttt{apply()}, which we showed can be used in combination with user-defined functions to provide a useful way to summarize data sets and apply functions iteratively to vectors or other R objects.

While this chapter may be a bit technical, we feel an understanding of how to write your own functions and how to use essential programming techniques like conditional statements and loops can greatly improve the efficiency of working with and analyzing forestry and ecological data. While R has a vast suite of packages that consist of all sorts of functions, having the ability to address a problem that you can't easily do using built-in R functions by using the programming techniques we have discussed can save you tremendous amounts of painful time that would have been spent searching the web for that specific R function you need (that may or may not exist). Further, as you progress in your education, job, or research, you will almost certainly encounter a situation where there is no R function to accomplish the task. The concepts we covered in this chapter provide you with the necessary tools to tackle those problems head on.

\hypertarget{exercisesFunctions}{%
\section{Exercises}\label{exercisesFunctions}}

\begin{exercise}
\protect\hypertarget{exr:functions1}{}\label{exr:functions1}Consider the \texttt{for} loop used in Section \ref{forLoops} to compute the AGB for each tree in the \texttt{pef\_p11} data set. Use the \texttt{apply()} function to accomplish the same task.
\end{exercise}

  \bibliography{book.bib,packages.bib}

\printindex
